You are the Karpathy-method teaching assistant, not Andrej Karpathy. Joshua's instructions remain authoritative. Do the teaching task directly rather than recursively delegating to yourself.

# Teach with the reviewed Karpathy method

Your first tool action on each teaching task must read wiki/karpathy/hot.md, followed by wiki/karpathy/rules.md, before writing or running code. Perform the task using these instructions. Read no more than five wiki pages per task, including hot and rules. Cite the source pages for claims about Karpathy. The collected sources are compiled with explicit text, payload, attribution and media limits.

For a code lesson: define done, show the smallest useful mechanism, predict the result before execution, run it, explain the observed result, demonstrate a meaningful isolated failure, fix and rerun, then ask the learner to reconstruct one step. Application files and dependencies belong in sibling ai-os-projects/.

Before presenting the answer, grade all seven rules with concrete pass/needs-improvement/not-applicable evidence: mechanism reconstruction, small baseline, prediction/run/comparison, counterexample/fix, relevant execution, explicit assumptions, justified simplicity. A non-code question may mark execution and broken-code demonstration not applicable.

Use ../ai-os-projects/karpathy-method-tools/run_gate.py --run with --file for edited Python files to preserve execution receipts. This helper traces executed Python files and hashes; an unrelated command does not prove the edited code ran. Rerun after edits. Codex project hooks are configured in .codex/hooks.json using codex_stop.py. New hook definitions must be reviewed and trusted through Codex /hooks before they run. Until activation is verified, enforce execution through this workflow and reviewed receipts; do not claim native blocking. Never bypass or fabricate hook trust. Preserve Joshua's instructions and voice.

Give the explanation, source links, assumptions/limits and seven-rule grades before the ending. End every answer with exactly these three sections, in order: What I ran; What came out; What I changed. New source intake uses the existing `ingest` skill; do not build a competing pipeline.

## developer

<multi_agent_role>You are an agent in a team of agents collaborating to complete a task.

You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents. All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.

When you provide a response in the final channel, that content is immediately delivered back to your parent agent.
In addition, your final answer may be read by a human, so ensure it is legible.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: NEW_TASK | MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
You may also see them addressed as to=/root/..., which indicates your identity is /root/...

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"`.</multi_agent_role>

These seven recurring patterns are a synthesis of reviewed public evidence, not Karpathy\'s endorsement, private mind or a claim that every historical implementation followed them. Each has two distinct original source artifacts with exact quotation support. Separate artifacts corroborate recurring advice; they do not establish independent experimental replication. Automatic-caption wording remains uncorrected and is not audio-verified.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidence** below describes his advice or demonstration. **Application** is our practical implementation and grading standard. Repository ownership, model-generated prompts, third-party contributions and syndicated copies were not used as sole proof of personal authorship. Joshua\'s instructions, identity and teaching voice remain authoritative.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 1. Explain and reconstruct the mechanism\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidence:** His study guidance distinguishes active reconstruction from passive understanding; his backprop article recommends implementation to solidify mechanism-level understanding.\\r\\n\\r\\n> Reading and understanding IS NOT the same as replicating\\r\\n\\r\\n[Study advice](../sources/karpathy/blog/undergraduate-study-advice.md), study section; [exact raw text](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/other-first-party/cs.stanford.edu_people_karpathy_advice.html.md). Publication date unknown.\\r\\n\\r\\n> get you to write backprop manually and help you solidify your understanding.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Yes you should understand backprop](../sources/karpathy/blog/understand-backprop.md), conclusion, 2016-12-19; [exact raw text](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/medium/karpathy.medium.com_yes-you-should-understand-backprop-e2f06eab496b.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n**Application:** Explain input, transformation and output using a small example, then invite the learner to reproduce or explain one step. Hands-on mechanism literacy does not require rebuilding every production dependency.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 2. Start with one correct small case and a baseline\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidence:** His recipe recommends getting a specific implementation working before generalizing. In Makemore he starts the neural pipeline with one word rather than the full dataset.\\r\\n\\r\\n> get that to work, and then generalize it later making sure that I get the same result.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Recipe](../sources/karpathy/blog/recipe.md), “generalize a special case,” 2019-04-25; [exact raw text](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/github-blog/karpathy.github.io_2019_04_25_recipe.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n> [01:06:43] all the words just yet because I want\\r\\n> [01:06:45] everything to be manageable. Uh so,\\r\\n> [01:06:47] let\'s just do the first word, which is\\r\\n\\r\\n[Makemore lecture](../sources/karpathy/lectures/pacmpygffxo.md), 2022-09-07; [exact raw captions](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/youtube/transcripts/PaCmpygFfXo.md). The preceding cue says not to take all words yet.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Application:** Establish one small end-to-end case and a baseline, then introduce one justified change at a time. A teaching toy and a production system have different success requirements. This rule does not rely on treating autoresearch\'s model-facing instructions as independently verified Karpathy authorship.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 3. Predict, run and compare\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidence:** His recipe describes hypotheses followed by validation or investigation; Makemore predicts normalized row sums and unchanged results before running the vectorized calculation.\\r\\n\\r\\n> make concrete hypotheses about what will happen and then either validate them with an experiment or investigate until we find some issue.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Recipe](../sources/karpathy/blog/recipe.md), “The recipe,” 2019-04-25; [exact raw text](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/github-blog/karpathy.github.io_2019_04_25_recipe.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n> [00:44:04] taking its sum. We expect this to be\\r\\n> [00:44:06] one.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Makemore lecture](../sources/karpathy/lectures/pacmpygffxo.md), normalization check, 2022-09-07; [exact raw captions](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/youtube/transcripts/PaCmpygFfXo.md). At 00:44:16 he runs the calculation; 00:44:19 reports the same result.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Application:** State the expected behavior before executing a meaningful check, report what happened, and explain any mismatch. Our requirement to write the prediction is an operational aid; the source establishes prediction and comparison rather than a universal written ritual. Expected output never substitutes for observed output.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 4. Expose a counterexample and repair its cause\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidence:** Micrograd exposes a repeated-node derivative bug with a tiny counterexample; the backprop article traces why clipping a forward error accidentally eliminates the needed gradient.\\r\\n\\r\\n> [01:22:39] so here\'s the simplest case that shows\\r\\n> [01:22:42] the bug\\r\\n\\r\\n[Micrograd lecture](../sources/karpathy/lectures/vmj-3s1tku0.md), repeated-node example, 2022-08-16; [exact raw captions](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/youtube/transcripts/VMj-3S1tku0.md). It expects derivative two, exposes overwritten contributions and repairs them with accumulation.\\r\\n\\r\\n> The problem is on line 291.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Backprop article](../sources/karpathy/blog/understand-backprop.md), DQN clipping example, 2016-12-19; [exact raw text](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/medium/karpathy.medium.com_yes-you-should-understand-backprop-e2f06eab496b.md). The subsequent explanation identifies zero gradients outside the clipping interval and replaces raw-error clipping with Huber loss.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Application:** Use a meaningful edge or failing case to make a cause inspectable, repair it and check the repaired behavior. An isolated deliberately broken teaching example is useful when appropriate; the evidence does not prescribe manufacturing a bug in every answer or breaking production.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 5. Verify the relevant behavior, not merely a successful run\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidence:** His coding notes recommend tests with explicit success criteria. The GPT-2 lecture compares optimized accumulation with a small reference and confirms equal gradients after correcting its normalization.\\r\\n\\r\\n> Get it to write tests first and then pass them.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Claude coding notes](../sources/karpathy/x/claude-coding-notes.md), 2026-01-26, post `2015883857489522876`; [raw ledger](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/x/posts.jsonl), selected own-author `text` field for that ID.\\r\\n\\r\\n> [02:43:32] that the U gradients are now identical\\r\\n\\r\\n[GPT-2 reproduction lecture](../sources/karpathy/lectures/l8prsuu81pu.md), reference comparison, 2024-06-09; [exact raw captions](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/youtube/transcripts/l8pRSuU81PU.md). The caption\'s “U” is preserved, not silently corrected.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Application:** Run checks that test the requested behavior against a reference, invariant or expected outcome. Report commands, results and untested limits. A failed actual run still ran but cannot justify “works.” Local checks do not certify live, production or device acceptance.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Historical counterevidence:** [Neuraltalk2\'s beam-search comment](../sources/karpathy/repos/supplemental/repos--karpathy--neuraltalk2--misc--LanguageModel-lua.md) says it is not fully unit-tested and accepts plausible outputs; [runq.c](../sources/karpathy/repos/final-three/repos--karpathy--llama2-c--runq-c.md) explicitly calls chat an unsafe proof of concept. These qualify any claim that rigorous verification characterized every past implementation. The rule is recurring advice and demonstration, not an unbroken biography.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 6. Make assumptions and uncertainty visible\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidence:** His coding notes criticize unchecked assumptions. Animals vs Ghosts explicitly leaves substantial uncertainty in its own conclusions.\\r\\n\\r\\n> the models make wrong assumptions on your behalf and just run along with them without checking.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Claude coding notes](../sources/karpathy/x/claude-coding-notes.md), 2026-01-26, post `2015883857489522876`; [raw ledger](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/x/posts.jsonl), selected own-author `text` field.\\r\\n\\r\\n> with double digit percent uncertainty\\r\\n\\r\\n[Animals vs Ghosts](../sources/karpathy/animals-vs-ghosts.md), his own “As for my take” discussion, 2025-10-01; [exact raw text](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/bearblog/karpathy.bearblog.dev_animals-vs-ghosts.md). This is his qualification, not the opening exposition of Sutton\'s view.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Application:** Expose material assumptions, contradictions and uncertainty; resolve what the available evidence can resolve, then ask about consequential remaining ambiguity. Keep dated capability claims attributed. The blog corroborates uncertainty disclosure, not a universal clarification protocol; our questioning procedure is an implementation choice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 7. Keep only complexity that earns its place\\r\\n\\r\\n**Evidence:** Microgpt pursues a minimal algorithmic teaching core; Calculator praises low dependency and operating burden. These are distinct essays about educational mechanism and product operation.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I cannot simplify this any further.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Microgpt](../sources/karpathy/blog/microgpt.md), opening, 2026-02-12; [exact raw text](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/github-blog/karpathy.github.io_2026_02_12_microgpt.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n> almost zero \\"dependency footprint\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n[I love calculator](../sources/karpathy/blog/calculator.md), self-contained artifact discussion, 2024-09-08; [exact raw text](../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/bearblog/karpathy.bearblog.dev_i-love-calculator.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n**Application:** Prefer a readable implementation with only the abstractions, dependencies and services the task needs. Preserve correctness while simplifying. Necessary integrations remain necessary, and minimal educational code is not automatically a production architecture. The [nanochat experiment log](../sources/karpathy/repos/final-three/repos--karpathy--nanochat--dev--log-md.md) records small-scale wins later reversed when target-scale wall-clock gains did not justify complexity; do not preserve an early success while omitting its reversal.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Quote and independence audit\\r\\n\\r\\nOn 2026-10-08 every displayed quotation above was checked as an exact substring of its linked raw artifact, including timestamped caption cues. X quotations were checked against the selected post\'s own-author text rather than quoted foreign text. The raw-file identity and hash remain on each source page; the X source uses a selected-text-field hash, not a ledger-file hash.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Rule | Distinct source identities | Independence result |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| 1 | Stanford personal study advice; Medium backprop article | Separate writings; shared author/theme, no syndicated duplicate |\\r\\n| 2 | 2019 Recipe; 2022 Makemore lecture `PaCmpygFfXo` | Article and separate teaching demonstration |\\r\\n| 3 | 2019 Recipe; 2022 Makemore lecture `PaCmpygFfXo` | Article and explicit prediction/check demonstration |\\r\\n| 4 | 2022 Micrograd lecture `VMj-3S1tku0`; 2016 backprop article | Distinct repeated-node and Q-error-clipping cases |\\r\\n| 5 | X post `2015883857489522876`; 2024 GPT-2 lecture `l8pRSuU81PU` | Advice and separate reference-comparison demonstration |\\r\\n| 6 | X post `2015883857489522876`; 2025 Animals vs Ghosts | Different subjects/artifacts, not the essay\'s syndicated X copy |\\r\\n| 7 | 2026 Microgpt; 2024 Calculator | Separate educational and product essays |\\r\\n\\r\\nThe same source can support multiple different rules; two passages within one source never count as two locations for a single rule. Confidence concerns recurrence and quotation support, not source completeness beyond the collected archive, factual truth of every source claim or successful execution here.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Grade the answer against observable evidence\\r\\n\\r\\nFor each rule mark pass, needs improvement, or not applicable, with one concrete reason. Do not award a pass for merely repeating a rule. Questions without code may have execution marked not applicable. Source-specific claims link to the source pages. Joshua\'s instructions, identity and teaching voice remain authoritative.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: [index](index.md), [hot](hot.md), [methods](methods.md), [execution record](../../research/karpathy-brain-2026-10-08/VIDEO-PROMPT-EXECUTION.md).\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 9.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Warning: truncated output (original token count: 11253)\nTotal output lines: 1\n\n{"chunk_id":"300748","wall_time_seconds":0.9102543,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":10677,"output":"---\\r\\nname: pr\\r\\ndescription: \\"Use when writing a PR body.\\"\\r\\nmetadata:\\r\\n  credits:\\r\\n    skill: show-me\\r\\n    author: Dex Horthy\\r\\n    organisation: Humanlayer\\r\\n    url: \\"https://github.com/humanlayer/skills/blob/main/plugins/show-me/skills/show-me/SKILL.md\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this template for writing the PR body:\\r\\n\\r\\n```markdown\\r\\n## Summary\\r\\n\\r\\n<diagram, diff-sketch, or tree>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Before:** <screenshot/output/failing test run>\\r\\n  **After:** <screenshot/output/passing test run>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Merge Danger\\r\\n\\r\\n**Door:** <one-way or two-way>\\r\\n\\r\\n<optional: description>\\r\\n\\r\\n**Blast Radius:** <one-word description>\\r\\n\\r\\n<optional: potential ramifications of merge>\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sections\\r\\n\\r\\nSkip all preambles and keep prose brief. Use the user\'s domain language from `GLOSSARY.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Summary\\r\\n\\r\\nPick the smallest view that makes the key point clear.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Show logic or an algorithm as pseudocode:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\non(save)\\r\\n  if content is unchanged\\r\\n    return cached result\\r\\n  write new content\\r\\n  return fresh result\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n- Show runtime control flow as a call tree:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nsubmitForm\\r\\n  createSession\\r\\n    persistPrompt\\r\\n    launchAgent\\r\\n  navigateToSession\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n- Show UI structure as a component tree, including state and module boundaries that matter:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\n<SessionPage> (apps/example/src/routes/session.tsx)\\r\\n  useSessionEvents()\\r\\n  <SessionToolbar>\\r\\n    <RunSkillButton> (packages/ui)\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n- Show file responsibility or a broad refactor as a shallow file tree:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nsrc/\\r\\n├── commands/       # parses user actions\\r\\n├── sessions/       # owns session state\\r\\n└── transport/      # sends API requests\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n- Show component interaction, control flow, or data flow with Mermaid:\\r\\n\\r\\n```mermaid\\r\\nsequenceDiagram\\r\\n    participant User\\r\\n    participant UI\\r\\n    participant Daemon\\r\\n    User->>UI: choose command\\r\\n    UI->>Daemon: send expanded prompt\\r\\n    Daemon-->>UI: stream result\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n- Use `diff` when the point is what changes and the surrounding shape already exists. Match the diff shape to the topic.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a component change:\\r\\n\\r\\n```diff\\r\\n <SessionPage>\\r\\n   useSessionEvents()\\r\\n   <SessionToolbar>\\r\\n+    <RunSkillButton />\\r\\n   <SessionTimeline>\\r\\n+    <SkillResultCard />\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a file-layout change:\\r\\n\\r\\n```diff\\r\\n src/\\r\\n ├── commands/\\r\\n+│   └── show-me.ts       # expands the slash command\\r\\n ├── sessions/\\r\\n-└── transport.ts\\r\\n+└── transport/\\r\\n+    ├── client.ts\\r\\n+    └── stream.ts\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a call-tree or call-stack change:\\r\\n\\r\\n```diff\\r\\n submitForm\\r\\n   createSession\\r\\n     persistPrompt\\r\\n+    expandSkillMention\\r\\n     launchAgent\\r\\n-  navigateToSession\\r\\n+  navigateToSession\\r\\n+    subscribeToEvents\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a state or control-flow change:\\r\\n\\r\\n```diff\\r\\n on(save)\\r\\n-  write content\\r\\n+  if content is unchanged\\r\\n+    return cached result\\r\\n+  write new content\\r\\n+  invalidate cache\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n- Show the whole block when most of it is new, when omitted context would hide ownership or order, or when the user needs a copyable target shape:\\r\\n\\r\\n```ts\\r\\nfunction expandSkill(command: string): string {\\r\\n  const skillName = command.slice(1);\\r\\n  return `use the ${skillName} skill`;\\r\\n}\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Guidance\\r\\n\\r\\nPlace each visual next to the short text it supports. Keep only the calls, files, props, states, and boundaries needed to answer the user\'s current question or the options to resolve the current discussion point.\\r\\n\\r\\nYou may use one of these, you may use several, it is unlikely you will use all of them. Use your judgement and don\'t overwhelm the user.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Evidence\\r\\n\\r\\nConcrete evidence that the change works. Show a before and after.\\r\\n\\r\\nScreenshots are S-tier - when the environment is set up for it and the change is visual.\\r\\n\\r\\nExecution-based evidence is A-tier. Test results, console output. Show the exact test that now fails and passes, using pseudocode.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Merge Danger\\r\\n\\r\\nDescribe whether it\'s a one-way or two-way door. You can walk back through two-way doors, but not one-way doors. A PR that is cheap to roll back is lower risk. Changes that involve destructive actions or hard-to-reverse decisions are one-way doors.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe blast radius is the potential impact or scope of the changes introduced by this PR. Consider all possibilities. Examples are layout shift, breakages for consumers, mobile responsiveness, etc.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: prompt-architect-refiner\\r\\ndescription: Use this skill when the user wants to improve, rewrite, sharpen, structure, debug, compress, expand, or create a high-quality prompt for Claude Chat, Claude Cowork, Claude Code, Gemini, ChatGPT, Lovable, NotebookLM, MCP workflows, repo audits, file organization, curriculum production, or app-building tasks. Trigger on phrases like prompt enhancer, prompt refiner, improve this prompt, make this top notch, god mode prompt, Claude Cowork prompt, Claude Code prompt, system instructions, custom instructions, skill instructions, rewrite this for Claude, make it clearer, or turn this messy request into a professional prompt.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Prompt Architect Refiner\\r\\n\\r\\nYou are a senior prompt architect. Your job is to transform rough, messy, incomplete, or overloaded user instructions into clear, powerful, execution-ready prompts.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe goal is not to make prompts longer. The goal is to make prompts more reliable.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Default behavior\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen the user gives a rough prompt and asks to improve it, produce a polished prompt that is ready to paste into the target AI tool.\\r\\n\\r\\nDefault output should be the improved prompt only unless the user asks for analysis, options, explanation, or a strategy note.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user asks for a concise version, compress aggressively while preserving the task, role, constraints, and output requirements.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user asks for a “god mode” prompt, do not create vague hype. Create a high-control prompt with role, goal, context, workflow, constraints, output format, quality checks, and stopping rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Core framework\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery strong prompt should answer these questions clearly:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Role: Who should the model act as?\\r\\n2. Goal: What result should it produce?\\r\\n3. Context: What background matters?\\r\\n4. Inputs: What material should it use?\\r\\n5. Constraints: What must it avoid or preserve?\\r\\n6. Workflow: What steps should it follow?\\r\\n7. Output: What exact format should it return?\\r\\n8. Quality bar: What makes the result excellent?\\r\\n9. Verification: How should it check its work?\\r\\n10. Stopping rule: Should it wait for approval before executing?\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this framework silently. Do not list it unless useful.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Prompt modes\\r\\n\\r\\nChoose the right mode based on the target tool.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Claude Chat mode\\r\\n\\r\\nUse for reasoning, writing, analysis, research synthesis, decision-making, and non-code workflows.\\r\\n\\r\\nStrong Claude Chat prompts should:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Define the role clearly\\r\\n- Give relevant context\\r\\n- State the desired output format\\r\\n- Include constraints and preferences\\r\\n- Ask for assumptions to be made when safe\\r\\n- Require uncertainty to be stated clearly\\r\\n- Avoid unnecessary tool or code instructions\\r\\n\\r\\n### Claude Cowork mode\\r\\n\\r\\nUse for planning, setup, file review, audits, project coordination, and multi-step workflows.\\r\\n\\r\\nStrong Claude Cowork prompts should:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Clarify Cowork’s role as strategist, reviewer, organizer, or setup assistant\\r\\n- Separate planning from execution\\r\\n- Require a first-pass audit or report before changes\\r\\n- Require approval before moving, deleting, overwriting, or restructuring files\\r\\n- Include logs, trackers, or decision tables when useful\\r\\n- Preserve source files unless the user explicitly says otherwise\\r\\n- Ask Cowork to recommend, not blindly execute\\r\\n\\r\\nFor Cowork, prefer this pattern:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Inspect\\r\\n2. Summarize reality\\r\\n3. Recommend options\\r\\n4. Wait for approval\\r\\n5. Execute only approved changes\\r\\n6. Report exact changes\\r\\n\\r\\n### Claude Code mode\\r\\n\\r\\nUse for codebase audits, bug fixes, refactors, UI work, tests, and app implementation.\\r\\n\\r\\nStrong Claude Code prompts should:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Require repo inspection before claims\\r\\n- Ban edits until after a plan is approved, unless the user explicitly requests direct execution\\r\\n- Require file-by-file change maps\\r\\n- Require tests, lint, type checks, or manual verification steps where possible\\r\\n- Require final reporting with changed files, validation results, and remaining risks\\r\\n- Avoid broad rewrites unless explicitly approved\\r\\n\\r\\nFor Claude Code, prefer this pattern:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Inspect the relevant files\\r\\n2. Identify likely causes\\r\\n3. Propose a surgical plan\\r\\n4. Wait for greenlight if requested\\r\\n5. Implement\\r\\n6. Run checks\\r\\n7. Report exact diffs and results\\r\\n\\r\\n### Lovable mode\\r\\n\\r\\nUse for app edits in Lovable where the model may change UI and database-linked behavior.\\r\\n\\r\\nStrong Lovable prompts should:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Specify the exact page, component, or user flow\\r\\n- Require no broad redesign unless requested\\r\\n- Preserve working behavior\\r\\n- Ask for a previewable UI change\\r\\n- Require mobile and desktop awareness\\r\\n- Include design language and interaction expectations\\r\\n\\r\\n### NotebookLM / source synthesis mode\\r\\n\\r\\nUse for source-grounded notes, summaries, flashcards, podcasts, and curriculum synthesis.\\r\\n\\r\\nStrong NotebookLM prompts should:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Require source-grounded claims\\r\\n- Separate what the source says from added teaching structure\\r\\n- Ask for gaps, conflicts, duplicates, and strongest source passages\\r\\n- Avoid inventing unsupported details\\r\\n- Produce structured outputs like notes, flashcards, drills, and gap reports\\r\\n\\r\\n## Enhancement rules\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen refining a prompt:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Preserve the user’s actual intent.\\r\\n- Fix grammar and clarity without changing the goal.\\r\\n- Remove rambling, repetition, and ambiguity.\\r\\n- Add missing constraints only when they are strongly implied.\\r\\n- Make the prompt operational, not motivational.\\r\\n- Prefer precise verbs: inspect, classify, extract, compare, rank, rewrite, verify, report.\\r\\n- Avoid vague verbs: improve, help, optimize, make better, handle everything, unless defined.\\r\\n- Add stopping rules when the task could modify files, code, databases, or external accounts.\\r\\n- Add source-of-truth rules when the task depends on repo files, Drive files, screenshots, documents, or recent facts.\\r\\n- Add output formats when the user will need to copy, paste, compare, or approve work.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Quality upgrades\\r\\n\\r\\nWhere useful, add these elements:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Decision table\\r\\n- File-by-file report\\r\\n- Risk list\\r\\n- Assumptions section\\r\\n- Exact next actions\\r\\n- Approval checkpoint\\r\\n- Before/after comparison\\r\\n- Rubric\\r\\n- Acceptance criteria\\r\\n- Validation checklist\\r\\n- Source-of-truth hierarchy\\r\\n- “Do not do” section\\r\\n\\r\\n## Anti-patterns to remove\\r\\n\\r\\nRemove or rewrite:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Repeated instructions\\r\\n- Contradictory instructions\\r\\n- Giant vague tasks with no output format\\r\\n- Hype words like “god mode” unless converted into concrete quality controls\\r\\n- Overly broad “do everything” language\\r\\n- Unclear references like “this,” “that,” “the thing,” unless context is obvious\\r\\n- Destructive permissions such as delete, overwrite, move, or deploy without a checkpoint\\r\\n- Instructions that ask the AI to claim it inspected something it has not actually inspected\\r\\n\\r\\n## Output formats\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user asks for the improved prompt only, output only the final prompt.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user asks for options, use:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Best version\\r\\n- Short version\\r\\n- Strict execution version\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user asks for a system instruction, output:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\n[system instruction text]\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user asks for a Claude Code prompt, output:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\n[Claude Code prompt]\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user asks for a Cowork prompt, output:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\n[Claude Cowork prompt]\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Joshua-specific preferences\\r\\n\\r\\nFor Joshua’s LSAT U and Claude workflows:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Be direct and practical.\\r\\n- Keep prompts structured but not bloated.\\r\\n- Use clear approval checkpoints.\\r\\n- Preserve source files unless explicit permission is given.\\r\\n- For LSAT U code work, require repo inspection before claims.\\r\\n- For LSAT content work, require folder-by-folder or file-by-file judgment before rewriting.\\r\\n- For Google Drive cleanup, recommend move/archive over delete.\\r\\n- For curriculum production, treat messy files as source material that may contain salvageable value.\\r\\n- For UI work, emphasize premium, non-generic, dark-glass, private-academy quality.\\r\\n- Avoid em dashes.\\r\\n- Avoid generic SaaS language.\\r\\n- Prefer output that can be pasted directly into Claude, Cowork, Claude Code, Lovable, or NotebookLM.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Final self-check\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore returning the final prompt, check:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Is the role clear?\\r\\n2. Is the task concrete?\\r\\n3. Is the source of truth clear?\\r\\n4. Are constraints explicit?\\r\\n5. Is the output format clear?\\r\\n6. Are approval checkpoints included when needed?\\r\\n7. Did the prompt avoid unnecessary fluff?\\r\\n8. Would a strong AI know exactly what to do next?\\r\\n\\r\\nReturn the refined prompt in the cleanest possible form.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: qa\\r\\ndescription: Interactive QA session where user reports bugs or issues conversationally, and the agent files GitHub issues. Explores the codebase in the background for context and domain language. Use when user wants to report bugs, do QA, file issues conversationally, or mentions \\"QA session\\".\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# QA Session\\r\\n\\r\\nRun an interactive QA session. The user describes problems they\'re encountering. You clarify, explore the codebase for context, and file GitHub issues that are durable, user-focused, and use the project\'s domain language.\\r\\n\\r\\n## For each issue the user raises\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Listen and lightly clarify\\r\\n\\r\\nLet the user describe the problem in their own words. Ask **at most 2-3 short clarifying questions** focused on:\\r\\n\\r\\n- What they expected vs what actually happened\\r\\n- Steps to reproduce (if not obvious)\\r\\n- Whether it\'s consistent or intermittent\\r\\n\\r\\nDo NOT over-interview. If the description is clear enough to file, move on.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Explore the codebase in the background\\r\\n\\r\\nWhile talking to the user, kick off an Agent (subagent_type=Explore) in the background to understand the relevant area. The goal is NOT to find a fix — it\'s to:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Learn the domain language used in that area (check UBIQUITOUS_LANGUAGE.md)\\r\\n- Understand what the feature is supposed to do\\r\\n- Identify the user-facing behavior boundary\\r\\n\\r\\nThis context helps you write a better issue — but the issue itself should NOT reference specific files, line numbers, or internal implementation details.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Assess scope: single issue or breakdown?\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore filing, decide whether this is a **single issue** or needs to be **broken down** into multiple issues.\\r\\n\\r\\nBreak down when:\\r\\n\\r\\n- The fix spans multiple independent areas (e.g. \\"the form validation is wrong AND the success message is missing AND the redirect is broken\\")\\r\\n- There are clearly separable concerns that different people could work on in parallel\\r\\n- The user describes something that has multiple distinct failure modes or symptoms\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep as a single issue when:\\r\\n\\r\\n- It\'s one behavior that\'s wrong in one place\\r\\n- The symptoms are all caused by the same root behavior\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4. File the GitHub issue(s)\\r\\n\\r\\nCreate issues with `gh issue create`. Do NOT ask the user to review first — just file and share URLs.\\r\\n\\r\\nIssues must be **durable** — they should still make sense after major refactors. Write from the user\'s perspective.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### For a single issue\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this template:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\n## What happened\\r\\n\\r\\n[Describe the actual behavior the user experienced, in plain language]\\r\\n\\r\\n## What I expected\\r\\n\\r\\n[Describe the expected behavior]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Steps to reproduce\\r\\n\\r\\n1. [Concrete, numbered steps a developer can follow]\\r\\n2. [Use domain terms from the codebase, not internal module names]\\r\\n3. [Include relevant inputs, flags, or configuration]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Additional context\\r\\n\\r\\n[Any extra observations from the user or from codebase exploration that help frame the issue — e.g. \\"this only happens when using the Docker layer, not the filesystem layer\\" — use domain language but don\'t cite files]\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n#### For a breakdown (multiple issues)\\r\\n\\r\\nCreate issues in dependency order (blockers first) so you can reference real issue numbers.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this template for each sub-issue:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\n## Parent issue\\r\\n\\r\\n#<parent-issue-number> (if you created a tracking issue) or \\"Reported during QA session\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n## What\'s wrong\\r\\n\\r\\n[Describe this specific behavior problem — just this slice, not the whole report]\\r\\n\\r\\n## What I expected\\r\\n\\r\\n[Expected behavior for this specific slice]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Steps to reproduce\\r\\n\\r\\n1. [Steps specific to THIS issue]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Blocked by\\r\\n\\r\\n- #<issue-number> (if this issue can\'t be fixed until another is resolved)\\r\\n\\r\\nOr \\"None — can start immediately\\" if no blockers.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Additional context\\r\\n\\r\\n[Any extra observations relevant to this slice]\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen creating a breakdown:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Prefer many thin issues over few thick ones** — each should be independently fixable and verifiable\\r\\n- **Mark blocking relationships honestly** — if issue B genuinely can\'t be tested until issue A is fixed, say so. If they\'re independent, mark both as \\"None — can start immediately\\"\\r\\n- **Create issues in dependency order** so you can reference real issue numbers in \\"Blocked by\\"\\r\\n- **Maximize parallelism** — the goal is that multiple people (or agents) can grab different issues simultaneously\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Rules for all issue bodies\\r\\n\\r\\n- **No file paths or line numbers** — these go stale\\r\\n- **Use the project\'s domain language** (check UBIQUITOUS_LANGUAGE.md if it exists)\\r\\n- **Describe behaviors, not code** — \\"the sync service fails to apply the patch\\" not \\"applyPatch() throws on line 42\\"\\r\\n- **Reproduction steps are mandatory** — if you can\'t determine them, ask the user\\r\\n- **Keep it concise** — a developer should be able to read the issue in 30 seconds\\r\\n\\r\\nAfter filing, print all issue URLs (with blocking relationships summarized) and ask: \\"Next issue, or are we done?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n### 5. Continue the session\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep going until the user says they\'re done. Each issue is independent — don\'t batch them.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: receiving-code-review\\r\\ndescription: Use when receiving code review feedback, before implementing suggestions, especially if feedback seems unclear or technically questionable - requires technical rigor and verification, not performative agreement or blind implementation\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Code Review Reception\\r\\n\\r\\n## Overview\\r\\n\\r\\nCode review requires technical evaluation, not emotional performance.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Core principle:** Verify before implementing. Ask before assuming. Technical correctness over social comfort.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The Response Pattern\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nWHEN receiving code review feedback:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. READ: Complete feedback without reacting\\r\\n2. UNDERSTAND: Restate requirement in own words (or ask)\\r\\n3. VERIFY: Check against codebase reality\\r\\n4. EVALUATE: Technically sound for THIS codebase?\\r\\n5. RESPOND: Technical acknowledg…1253 tokens truncated…cy code\\"\\r\\n❌ \\"You\'re absolutely right! Let me remove that...\\"\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**Technical Verification (Good):**\\r\\n```\\r\\nReviewer: \\"Remove legacy code\\"\\r\\n✅ \\"Checking... build target is 10.15+, this API needs 13+. Need legacy for backward compat. Current impl has wrong bundle ID - fix it or drop pre-13 support?\\"\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**YAGNI (Good):**\\r\\n```\\r\\nReviewer: \\"Implement proper metrics tracking with database, date filters, CSV export\\"\\r\\n✅ \\"Grepped codebase - nothing calls this endpoint. Remove it (YAGNI)? Or is there usage I\'m missing?\\"\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**Unclear Item (Good):**\\r\\n```\\r\\nyour human partner: \\"Fix items 1-6\\"\\r\\nYou understand 1,2,3,6. Unclear on 4,5.\\r\\n✅ \\"Understand 1,2,3,6. Need clarification on 4 and 5 before implementing.\\"\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## GitHub Thread Replies\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen replying to inline review comments on GitHub, reply in the comment thread (`gh api repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/{pr}/comments/{id}/replies`), not as a top-level PR comment.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The Bottom Line\\r\\n\\r\\n**External feedback = suggestions to evaluate, not orders to follow.**\\r\\n\\r\\nVerify. Question. Then implement.\\r\\n\\r\\nNo performative agreement. Technical rigor always.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: redesign-existing-projects\\r\\ndescription: Upgrades existing websites and apps to premium quality. Audits current design, identifies generic AI patterns, and applies high-end design standards without breaking functionality. Works with any CSS framework or vanilla CSS.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Redesign Skill\\r\\n\\r\\n## How This Works\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen applied to an existing project, follow this sequence:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Scan** — Read the codebase. Identify the framework, styling method (Tailwind, vanilla CSS, styled-components, etc.), and current design patterns.\\r\\n2. **Diagnose** — Run through the audit below. List every generic pattern, weak point, and missing state you find.\\r\\n3. **Fix** — Apply targeted upgrades working with the existing stack. Do not rewrite from scratch. Improve what\'s there.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Design Audit\\r\\n\\r\\n### Typography\\r\\n\\r\\nCheck for these problems and fix them:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Browser default fonts or Inter everywhere.** Replace with a font that has character. Good options: `Geist`, `Outfit`, `Cabinet Grotesk`, `Satoshi`. For editorial/creative projects, pair a serif header with a sans-serif body.\\r\\n- **Headlines lack presence.** Increase size for display text, tighten letter-spacing, reduce line-height. Headlines should feel heavy and intentional.\\r\\n- **Body text too wide.** Limit paragraph width to roughly 65 characters. Increase line-height for readability.\\r\\n- **Only Regular (400) and Bold (700) weights used.** Introduce Medium (500) and SemiBold (600) for more subtle hierarchy.\\r\\n- **Numbers in proportional font.** Use a monospace font or enable tabular figures (`font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums`) for data-heavy interfaces.\\r\\n- **Missing letter-spacing adjustments.** Use negative tracking for large headers, positive tracking for small caps or labels.\\r\\n- **All-caps subheaders everywhere.** Try lowercase italics, sentence case, or small-caps instead.\\r\\n- **Orphaned words.** Single words sitting alone on the last line. Fix with `text-wrap: balance` or `text-wrap: pretty`.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Color and Surfaces\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Pure `#000000` background.** Replace with off-black, dark charcoal, or tinted dark (`#0a0a0a`, `#121212`, or a dark navy).\\r\\n- **Oversaturated accent colors.** Keep saturation below 80%. Desaturate accents so they blend with neutrals instead of screaming.\\r\\n- **More than one accent color.** Pick one. Remove the rest. Consistency beats variety.\\r\\n- **Mixing warm and cool grays.** Stick to one gray family. Tint all grays with a consistent hue (warm or cool, not both).\\r\\n- **Purple/blue \\"AI gradient\\" aesthetic.** This is the most common AI design fingerprint. Replace with neutral bases and a single, considered accent.\\r\\n- **Generic `box-shadow`.** Tint shadows to match the background hue. Use colored shadows (e.g., dark blue shadow on a blue background) instead of pure black at low opacity.\\r\\n- **Flat design with zero texture.** Add subtle noise, grain, or micro-patterns to backgrounds. Pure flat vectors feel sterile.\\r\\n- **Perfectly even gradients.** Break the uniformity with radial gradients, noise overlays, or mesh gradients instead of standard linear 45-degree fades.\\r\\n- **Inconsistent lighting direction.** Audit all shadows to ensure they suggest a single, consistent light source.\\r\\n- **Random dark sections in a light mode page (or vice versa).** A single dark-background section breaking an otherwise light page looks like a copy-paste accident. Either commit to a full dark mode or keep a consistent background tone throughout. If contrast is needed, use a slightly darker shade of the same palette — not a sudden jump to `#111` in the middle of a cream page.\\r\\n- **Empty, flat sections with no visual depth.** Sections that are just text on a plain background feel unfinished. Add high-quality background imagery (blurred, overlaid, or masked), subtle patterns, or ambient gradients. Use reliable placeholder sources like `https://picsum.photos/seed/{name}/1920/1080` when real assets are not available. Experiment with background images behind hero sections, feature blocks, or CTAs — even a subtle full-width photo at low opacity adds presence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Layout\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Everything centered and symmetrical.** Break symmetry with offset margins, mixed aspect ratios, or left-aligned headers over centered content.\\r\\n- **Three equal card columns as feature row.** This is the most generic AI layout. Replace with a 2-column zig-zag, asymmetric grid, horizontal scroll, or masonry layout.\\r\\n- **Using `height: 100vh` for full-screen sections.** Replace with `min-height: 100dvh` to prevent layout jumping on mobile browsers (iOS Safari viewport bug).\\r\\n- **Complex flexbox percentage math.** Replace with CSS Grid for reliable multi-column structures.\\r\\n- **No max-width container.** Add a container constraint (around 1200-1440px) with auto margins so content doesn\'t stretch edge-to-edge on wide screens.\\r\\n- **Cards of equal height forced by flexbox.** Allow variable heights or use masonry when content varies in length.\\r\\n- **Uniform border-radius on everything.** Vary the radius: tighter on inner elements, softer on containers.\\r\\n- **No overlap or depth.** Elements sit flat next to each other. Use negative margins to create layering and visual depth.\\r\\n- **Symmetrical vertical padding.** Top and bottom padding are always identical. Adjust optically — bottom padding often needs to be slightly larger.\\r\\n- **Dashboard always has a left sidebar.** Try top navigation, a floating command menu, or a collapsible panel instead.\\r\\n- **Missing whitespace.** Double the spacing. Let the design breathe. Dense layouts work for data dashboards, not for marketing pages.\\r\\n- **Buttons not bottom-aligned in card groups.** When cards have different content lengths, CTAs end up at random heights. Pin buttons to the bottom of each card so they form a clean horizontal line regardless of content above.\\r\\n- **Feature lists starting at different vertical positions.** In pricing tables or comparison cards, the list of features should start at the same Y position across all columns. Use consistent spacing above the list or fixed-height title/price blocks.\\r\\n- **Inconsistent vertical rhythm in side-by-side elements.** When placing cards, columns, or panels next to each other, align shared elements (titles, descriptions, prices, buttons) across all items. Misaligned baselines make the layout look broken.\\r\\n- **Mathematical alignment that looks optically wrong.** Centering by the math doesn\'t always look centered to the eye. Icons next to text, play buttons in circles, or text in buttons often need 1-2px optical adjustments to feel right.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Interactivity and States\\r\\n\\r\\n- **No hover states on buttons.** Add background shift, slight scale, or translate on hover.\\r\\n- **No active/pressed feedback.** Add a subtle `scale(0.98)` or `translateY(1px)` on press to simulate a physical click.\\r\\n- **Instant transitions with zero duration.** Add smooth transitions (200-300ms) to all interactive elements.\\r\\n- **Missing focus ring.** Ensure visible focus indicators for keyboard navigation. This is an accessibility requirement, not optional.\\r\\n- **No loading states.** Replace generic circular spinners with skeleton loaders that match the layout shape.\\r\\n- **No empty states.** An empty dashboard showing nothing is a missed opportunity. Design a composed \\"getting started\\" view.\\r\\n- **No error states.** Add clear, inline error messages for forms. Do not use `window.alert()`.\\r\\n- **Dead links.** Buttons that link to `#`. Either link to real destinations or visually disable them.\\r\\n- **No indication of current page in navigation.** Style the active nav link differently so users know where they are.\\r\\n- **Scroll jumping.** Anchor clicks jump instantly. Add `scroll-behavior: smooth`.\\r\\n- **Animations using `top`, `left`, `width`, `height`.** Switch to `transform` and `opacity` for GPU-accelerated, smooth animation.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Content\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Generic names like \\"John Doe\\" or \\"Jane Smith\\".** Use diverse, realistic-sounding names.\\r\\n- **Fake round numbers like `99.99%`, `50%`, `$100.00`.** Use organic, messy data: `47.2%`, `$99.00`, `+1 (312) 847-1928`.\\r\\n- **Placeholder company names like \\"Acme Corp\\", \\"Nexus\\", \\"SmartFlow\\".** Invent contextual, believable brand names.\\r\\n- **AI copywriting cliches.** Never use \\"Elevate\\", \\"Seamless\\", \\"Unleash\\", \\"Next-Gen\\", \\"Game-changer\\", \\"Delve\\", \\"Tapestry\\", or \\"In the world of...\\". Write plain, specific language.\\r\\n- **Exclamation marks in success messages.** Remove them. Be confident, not loud.\\r\\n- **\\"Oops!\\" error messages.** Be direct: \\"Connection failed. Please try again.\\"\\r\\n- **Passive voice.** Use active voice: \\"We couldn\'t save your changes\\" instead of \\"Mistakes were made.\\"\\r\\n- **All blog post dates identical.** Randomize dates to appear real.\\r\\n- **Same avatar image for multiple users.** Use unique assets for every distinct person.\\r\\n- **Lorem Ipsum.** Never use placeholder latin text. Write real draft copy.\\r\\n- **Title Case On Every Header.** Use sentence case instead.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Component Patterns\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Generic card look (border + shadow + white background).** Remove the border, or use only background color, or use only spacing. Cards should exist only when elevation communicates hierarchy.\\r\\n- **Always one filled button + one ghost button.** Add text links or tertiary styles to reduce visual noise.\\r\\n- **Pill-shaped \\"New\\" and \\"Beta\\" badges.** Try square badges, flags, or plain text labels.\\r\\n- **Accordion FAQ sections.** Use a side-by-side list, searchable help, or inline progressive disclosure.\\r\\n- **3-card carousel testimonials with dots.** Replace with a masonry wall, embedded social posts, or a single rotating quote.\\r\\n- **Pricing table with 3 towers.** Highlight the recommended tier with color and emphasis, not just extra height.\\r\\n- **Modals for everything.** Use inline editing, slide-over panels, or expandable sections instead of popups for simple actions.\\r\\n- **Avatar circles exclusively.** Try squircles or rounded squares for a less generic look.\\r\\n- **Light/dark toggle always a sun/moon switch.** Use a dropdown, system preference detection, or integrate it into settings.\\r\\n- **Footer link farm with 4 columns.** Simplify. Focus on main navigational paths and legally required links.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Iconography\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Lucide or Feather icons exclusively.** These are the \\"default\\" AI icon choice. Use Phosphor, Heroicons, or a custom set for differentiation.\\r\\n- **Rocketship for \\"Launch\\", shield for \\"Security\\".** Replace cliche metaphors with less obvious icons (bolt, fingerprint, spark, vault).\\r\\n- **Inconsistent stroke widths across icons.** Audit all icons and standardize to one stroke weight.\\r\\n- **Missing favicon.** Always include a branded favicon.\\r\\n- **Stock \\"diverse team\\" photos.** Use real team photos, candid shots, or a consistent illustration style instead of uncanny stock imagery.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Code Quality\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Div soup.** Use semantic HTML: `<nav>`, `<main>`, `<article>`, `<aside>`, `<section>`.\\r\\n- **Inline styles mixed with CSS classes.** Move all styling to the project\'s styling system.\\r\\n- **Hardcoded pixel widths.** Use relative units (`%`, `rem`, `em`, `max-width`) for flexible layouts.\\r\\n- **Missing alt text on images.** Describe image content for screen readers. Never leave `alt=\\"\\"` or `alt=\\"image\\"` on meaningful images.\\r\\n- **Arbitrary z-index values like `9999`.** Establish a clean z-index scale in the theme/variables.\\r\\n- **Commented-out dead code.** Remove all debug artifacts before shipping.\\r\\n- **Import hallucinations.** Check that every import actually exists in `package.json` or the project dependencies.\\r\\n- **Missing meta tags.** Add proper `<title>`, `description`, `og:image`, and social sharing meta tags.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Strategic Omissions (What AI Typically Forgets)\\r\\n\\r\\n- **No legal links.** Add privacy policy and terms of service links in the footer.\\r\\n- **No \\"back\\" navigation.** Dead ends in user flows. Every page needs a way back.\\r\\n- **No custom 404 page.** Design a helpful, branded \\"page not found\\" experience.\\r\\n- **No form validation.** Add client-side validation for emails, required fields, and format checks.\\r\\n- **No \\"skip to content\\" link.** Essential for keyboard users. Add a hidden skip-link.\\r\\n- **No cookie consent.** If required by jurisdiction, add a compliant consent banner.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Upgrade Techniques\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen upgrading a project, pull from these high-impact techniques to replace generic patterns:\\r\\n\\r\\n### Typography Upgrades\\r\\n- **Variable font animation.** Interpolate weight or width on scroll or hover for text that feels alive.\\r\\n- **Outlined-to-fill transitions.** Text starts as a stroke outline and fills with color on scroll entry or interaction.\\r\\n- **Text mask reveals.** Large typography acting as a window to video or animated imagery behind it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Layout Upgrades\\r\\n- **Broken grid / asymmetry.** Elements that deliberately ignore column structure — overlapping, bleeding off-screen, or offset with calculated randomness.\\r\\n- **Whitespace maximization.** Aggressive use of negative space to force focus on a single element.\\r\\n- **Parallax card stacks.** Sections that stick and physically stack over each other during scroll.\\r\\n- **Split-screen scroll.** Two halves of the screen sliding in opposite directions.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Motion Upgrades\\r\\n- **Smooth scroll with inertia.** Decouple scrolling from browser defaults for a heavier, cinematic feel.\\r\\n- **Staggered entry.** Elements cascade in with slight delays, combining Y-axis translation with opacity fade. Never mount everything at once.\\r\\n- **Spring physics.** Replace linear easing with spring-based motion for a natural, weighty feel on all interactive elements.\\r\\n- **Scroll-driven reveals.** Content entering through expanding masks, wipes, or draw-on SVG paths tied to scroll progress.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Surface Upgrades\\r\\n- **True glassmorphism.** Go beyond `backdrop-filter: blur`. Add a 1px inner border and a subtle inner shadow to simulate edge refraction.\\r\\n- **Spotlight borders.** Card borders that illuminate dynamically under the cursor.\\r\\n- **Grain and noise overlays.** A fixed, pointer-events-none overlay with subtle noise to break digital flatness.\\r\\n- **Colored, tinted shadows.** Shadows that carry the hue of the background rather than using generic black.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Fix Priority\\r\\n\\r\\nApply changes in this order for maximum visual impact with minimum risk:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Font swap** — biggest instant improvement, lowest risk\\r\\n2. **Color palette cleanup** — remove clashing or oversaturated colors\\r\\n3. **Hover and active states** — makes the interface feel alive\\r\\n4. **Layout and spacing** — proper grid, max-width, consistent padding\\r\\n5. **Replace generic components** — swap cliche patterns for modern alternatives\\r\\n6. **Add loading, empty, and error states** — makes it feel finished\\r\\n7. **Polish typography scale and spacing** — the premium final touch\\r\\n\\r\\n## Rules\\r\\n\\r\\n- Work with the existing tech stack. Do not migrate frameworks or styling libraries.\\r\\n- Do not break existing functionality. Test after every change.\\r\\n- Before importing any new library, check the project\'s dependency file first.\\r\\n- If the project uses Tailwind, check the version (v3 vs v4) before modifying config.\\r\\n- If the project has no framework, use vanilla CSS.\\r\\n- Keep changes reviewable and focused. Small, targeted improvements over big rewrites.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: requesting-code-review\\r\\ndescription: Use when completing tasks, implementing major features, or before merging to verify work meets requirements\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Requesting Code Review\\r\\n\\r\\nDispatch a code reviewer subagent to catch issues before they cascade. The reviewer gets precisely crafted context for evaluation — never your session\'s history. This keeps the reviewer focused on the work product, not your thought process, and preserves your own context for continued work.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Core principle:** Review early, review often.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When to Request Review\\r\\n\\r\\n**Mandatory:**\\r\\n- After each task in subagent-driven development\\r\\n- After completing major feature\\r\\n- Before merge to main\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optional but valuable:**\\r\\n- When stuck (fresh perspective)\\r\\n- Before refactoring (baseline check)\\r\\n- After fixing complex bug\\r\\n\\r\\n## How to Request\\r\\n\\r\\n**1. Get git SHAs:**\\r\\n```bash\\r\\nBASE_SHA=$(git rev-parse HEAD~1)  # or origin/main\\r\\nHEAD_SHA=$(git rev-parse HEAD)\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**2. Dispatch code reviewer subagent:**\\r\\n\\r\\nDispatch a `general-purpose` subagent, filling the template at [code-reviewer.md](code-reviewer.md)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Placeholders:**\\r\\n- `{DESCRIPTION}` - Brief summary of what you built\\r\\n- `{PLAN_OR_REQUIREMENTS}` - What it should do\\r\\n- `{BASE_SHA}` - Starting commit\\r\\n- `{HEAD_SHA}` - Ending commit\\r\\n\\r\\n**3. Act on feedback:**\\r\\n- Fix Critical issues immediately\\r\\n- Fix Important issues before proceeding\\r\\n- Note Minor issues for later\\r\\n- Push back if reviewer is wrong (with reasoning)\\r\\n\\r\\n## Example\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\n[Just completed Task 2: Add verification function]\\r\\n\\r\\nYou: Let me request code review before proceeding.\\r\\n\\r\\nBASE_SHA=$(git log --oneline | grep \\"Task 1\\" | head -1 | awk \'{print $1}\')\\r\\nHEAD_SHA=$(git rev-parse HEAD)\\r\\n\\r\\n[Dispatch code reviewer subagent]\\r\\n  DESCRIPTION: Added verifyIndex() and repairIndex() with 4 issue types\\r\\n  PLAN_OR_REQUIREMENTS: Task 2 from docs/superpowers/plans/deployment-plan.md\\r\\n  BASE_SHA: a7981ec\\r\\n  HEAD_SHA: 3df7661\\r\\n\\r\\n[Subagent returns]:\\r\\n  Strengths: Clean architecture, real tests\\r\\n  Issues:\\r\\n    Important: Missing progress indicators\\r\\n    Minor: Magic number (100) for reporting interval\\r\\n  Assessment: Ready to proceed\\r\\n\\r\\nYou: [Fix progress indicators]\\r\\n[Continue to Task 3]\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Integration with Workflows\\r\\n\\r\\n**Subagent-Driven Development:**\\r\\n- Review after EACH task\\r\\n- Catch issues before they compound\\r\\n- Fix before moving to next task\\r\\n\\r\\n**Executing Plans:**\\r\\n- Review after each task or at natural checkpoints\\r\\n- Get feedback, apply, continue\\r\\n\\r\\n**Ad-Hoc Development:**\\r\\n- Review before merge\\r\\n- Review when stuck\\r\\n\\r\\n## Red Flags\\r\\n\\r\\n**Never:**\\r\\n- Skip review because \\"it\'s simple\\"\\r\\n- Ignore Critical issues\\r\\n- Proceed with unfixed Important issues\\r\\n- Argue with valid technical feedback\\r\\n\\r\\n**If reviewer wrong:**\\r\\n- Push back with technical reasoning\\r\\n- Show code/tests that prove it works\\r\\n- Request clarification\\r\\n\\r\\nSee template at: [code-reviewer.md](code-reviewer.md)\\r\\n"}'}]
