This is a stateful request - they intend to learn the topic over multiple sessions.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Teaching Workspace\\r\\n\\r\\nTreat the current directory as a teaching workspace. Workspace paths (`./lessons/` and the rest) resolve from the directory `/teach` was run in; only the `*-FORMAT.md` links resolve from this skill\'s folder. The state of their learning is captured in this directory in several files:\\r\\n\\r\\n- `MISSION.md`: A document capturing the _reason_ the user is interested in the topic. This should be used to ground all teaching. Use the format in [MISSION-FORMAT.md](./MISSION-FORMAT.md).\\r\\n- `./reference/*.html`: A directory of reference materials. These are the compressed learnings from the lessons - cheat sheets, reference algorithms, syntax, yoga poses, glossaries. They are the raw units of learning. They should be beautiful documents which print out well, and are designed for quick reference.\\r\\n- `RESOURCES.md`: A list of resources which can be explored to ground your teaching in contextual knowledge, or to acquire knowledge and wisdom. Use the format in [RESOURCES-FORMAT.md](./RESOURCES-FORMAT.md).\\r\\n- `./learning-records/*.md`: A directory of learning records, which capture what the user has learned. These are loosely equivalent to architectural decision records in software development - they capture non-obvious lessons and key insights that may need to be revised later, or drive future sessions. These should be used to calculate the zone of proximal development. They are titled `0001-<dash-case-name>.md`, where the number increments each time. Use the format in [LEARNING-RECORD-FORMAT.md](./LEARNING-RECORD-FORMAT.md).\\r\\n- `./lessons/*.html`: A directory of lessons. A **lesson** is a single, self-contained HTML output that teaches one tightly-scoped thing tied to the mission. This is the primary unit of teaching in this workspace.\\r\\n- `./assets/*`: Reusable **components** shared across lessons. See [Assets](#assets).\\r\\n- `NOTES.md`: A scratchpad for you to jot down user preferences, or working notes.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Philosophy\\r\\n\\r\\nTo learn at a deep level, the user needs three things:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Knowledge**, captured from high-quality, high-trust resources\\r\\n- **Skills**, acquired through highly-relevant interactive lessons devised by you, based on the knowledge\\r\\n- **Wisdom**, which comes from interacting with other learners and practitioners\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore the `RESOURCES.md` is well-populated, your focus should be to find high-quality resources which will help the user acquire knowledge. Never trust your parametric knowledge.\\r\\n\\r\\nSome topics may require more skills than knowledge. Learning more about theoretical physics might be more knowledge-based. For yoga, more skills-based.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fluency vs Storage Strength\\r\\n\\r\\nYou should be careful to split between two types of learning:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Fluency strength**: in-the-moment retrieval of knowledge\\r\\n- **Storage strength**: long-term retention of knowledge\\r\\n\\r\\nFluency can give the user an illusory sense of mastery, but storage strength is the real goal. Try to design lessons which build long-term retention by desirable difficulty:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Using retrieval practice (recall from memory)\\r\\n- Spacing (distributing practice over time)\\r\\n- Interleaving (mixing up different but related topics in practice - for skills practice only)\\r\\n\\r\\n## Lessons\\r\\n\\r\\nA lesson is the main thing you produce: the unit in which knowledge and skills reach the user. Each lesson is one self-contained HTML file, saved to `./lessons/` and titled `0001-<dash-case-name>.html` where the number increments each time.\\r\\n\\r\\nA lesson should be **beautiful**, with clean, readable typography and layout, since the user will return to these later to review. Think Tufte.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe lesson should be short, and completable very quickly. Learners\' working memory is very small, and we need to stay within it. But each lesson should give the user a single tangible win that they can build on. It should be directly tied to the mission, and should be in the user\'s zone of proximal development.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf possible, open the lesson file for the user by running a CLI command.\\r\\n\\r\\nEach lesson should link via HTML anchors to other lessons and reference documents.\\r\\n\\r\\nEach lesson should recommend a primary source for the user to read or watch. This should be the most high-quality, high-trust resource you found on the topic.\\r\\n\\r\\nEach lesson should contain a reminder to ask followup questions to the agent. The agent is their teacher, and can assist with anything that\'s unclear.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Assets\\r\\n\\r\\nLessons are built from reusable **components**, stored in `./assets/`: stylesheets, quiz widgets, simulators, diagram helpers, and anything else a second lesson could reuse.\\r\\n\\r\\nReuse is the default, not the exception. Before authoring a lesson, read `./assets/` and build from the components already there. When a lesson needs something new and reusable, write it as a component in `./assets/` and link to it; never inline code a future lesson would duplicate.\\r\\n\\r\\nA shared stylesheet is the first component every workspace earns: every lesson links it, so the lessons look like one consistent course rather than a pile of one-offs. As the workspace grows, so should the component library.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The Mission\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery lesson should be tied into the mission - the reason that the user is interested in learning about the topic.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user is unclear about the mission, or the `MISSION.md` is not populated, your first job should be to question the user on why they want to learn this.\\r\\n\\r\\nFailing to understand the mission will mean knowledge acquisition is not grounded in real-world goals. Lessons will feel too abstract. You will have no way of judging what the user should do next.\\r\\n\\r\\nMissions may change as the user develops more skills and knowledge. This is normal - make sure to update the `MISSION.md` and add a learning record to capture the change. Confirm with the user before changing the mission.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Zone Of Proximal Development\\r\\n\\r\\nEach lesson, the user should always feel as if they are being challenged \'just enough\'.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe user may specify an exact thing they want to learn. If they don\'t, figure out their zone of proximal development by:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Reading their `learning-records`\\r\\n- Figuring out the right thing to teach them based on their mission\\r\\n- Teach the most relevant thing that fits in their zone of proximal development\\r\\n\\r\\n## Knowledge\\r\\n\\r\\nLessons should be designed around a skill the user is going to learn. The knowledge in the lesson should be only what\'s required to acquire that skill. You teach the knowledge first, then get the user to practice the skills via an interactive feedback loop.\\r\\n\\r\\nKnowledge should first be gathered from trusted resources. Use `RESOURCES.md` to keep track of them. Lessons should be littered with citations - links to external resources to back up any claim made. This increases the trustworthiness of the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor acquiring knowledge, difficulty is the enemy. It eats working memory you need for understanding.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Skills\\r\\n\\r\\nIf knowledge is all about acquisition, skills are about durability and flexibility. Make the knowledge stick.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor skill acquisition, difficulty is the tool. Effortful retrieval is what builds storage strength. Skills should be taught through interactive lessons. There are several tools at your disposal:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Interactive lessons, using quizzes and light in-browser tasks\\r\\n- Lessons which guide the user through a list of real-world steps to take (for instance, yoga poses)\\r\\n\\r\\nEach of these should be based on a **feedback loop**, where the user receives feedback on their performance. This feedback loop should be as tight as possible, giving feedback immediately - and ideally automatically.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor quizzes, each answer should be exactly the same number of words (and characters, if possible). Vary which position holds the correct answer across questions. Don\'t give the user any clues about the answer through formatting or order.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Acquiring Wisdom\\r\\n\\r\\nWisdom comes from true real-world interaction - testing your skills outside the learning environment.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen the user asks a question that appears to require wisdom, your default posture should be to attempt to answer - but to ultimately delegate to a **community**.\\r\\n\\r\\nA community is a place (online or offline) where the user can test their skills in the real world. This might be a forum, a subreddit, a real-world class (budget permitting) or a local interest group.\\r\\n\\r\\nYou should attempt to find high-reputation communities the user can join. If the user expresses a preference that they don\'t want to join a community, respect it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Reference Documents\\r\\n\\r\\nWhile creating lessons, you should also create reference documents. Lessons can reference these documents - they are useful for tracking raw units of knowledge useful across lessons.\\r\\n\\r\\nLessons will rarely be revisited later - reference documents will be. They should be the compressed essence of the lesson, in a format designed for quick reference.\\r\\n\\r\\nSome learning topics lend themselves to reference:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Syntax and code snippets for programming\\r\\n- Algorithms and flowcharts for processes\\r\\n- Yoga poses and sequences for yoga\\r\\n- Exercises and routines for fitness\\r\\n- Glossaries for any topic with its own nomenclature\\r\\n\\r\\nGlossaries, in particular, are an essential reference. Once one is created, it should be adhered to in every lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n## `NOTES.md`\\r\\n\\r\\nThe user will sometimes express preferences of how they want to be taught, or things you should keep in mind. This is the place to record those preferences, so you can refer back to them when designing lessons or working with the user.\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"Get-Content skills/claude/subagent-driven-development/SKILL.md | Select-Object -Skip 275 -First 67",max_output_tokens:2500}));

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 8.3 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"29e188","wall_time_seconds":0.3382887,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":481,"output":"\\r\\n[Read plan file once: docs/superpowers/plans/feature-plan.md]\\r\\n[Create todos for all tasks]\\r\\n\\r\\nTask 1: Hook installation script\\r\\n\\r\\n[Run task-brief for Task 1; dispatch implementer with brief + report paths + context]\\r\\n\\r\\nImplementer: \\"Before I begin - should the hook be installed at user or system level?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nYou: \\"User level (~/.config/superpowers/hooks/)\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nImplementer: \\"Got it. Implementing now...\\"\\r\\n[Later] Implementer:\\r\\n  - Implemented install-hook command\\r\\n  - Added tests, 5/5 passing\\r\\n  - Self-review: Found I missed --force flag, added it\\r\\n  - Committed\\r\\n\\r\\n[Run review-package, dispatch task reviewer with the printed path]\\r\\nTask reviewer: Spec ✅ - all requirements met, nothing extra.\\r\\n  Strengths: Good test coverage, clean. Issues: None. Task quality: Approved.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Mark Task 1 complete]\\r\\n\\r\\nTask 2: Recovery modes\\r\\n\\r\\n[Run task-brief for Task 2; dispatch implementer with brief + report paths + context]\\r\\n\\r\\nImplementer: [No questions, proceeds]\\r\\nImplementer:\\r\\n  - Added verify/repair modes\\r\\n  - 8/8 tests passing\\r\\n  - Self-review: All good\\r\\n  - Committed\\r\\n\\r\\n[Run review-package, dispatch task reviewer with the printed path]\\r\\nTask reviewer: Spec ❌:\\r\\n  - Missing: Progress reporting (spec says \\"report every 100 items\\")\\r\\n  - Extra: Added --json flag (not requested)\\r\\n  Issues (Important): Magic number (100)\\r\\n\\r\\n[Dispatch fix subagent with all findings]\\r\\nFixer: Removed --json flag, added progress reporting, extracted PROGRESS_INTERVAL constant\\r\\n\\r\\n[Task reviewer reviews again]\\r\\nTask reviewer: Spec ✅. Task quality: Approved.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Mark Task 2 complete]\\r\\n\\r\\n...\\r\\n\\r\\n[After all tasks]\\r\\n[Dispatch final code-reviewer]\\r\\nFinal reviewer: All requirements met, ready to merge\\r\\n\\r\\nDone!\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Advantages\\r\\n\\r\\n**vs. Manual execution:**\\r\\n- Subagents follow TDD naturally\\r\\n- Fresh context per task (no confusion)\\r\\n- Parallel-safe (subagents don\'t interfere)\\r\\n- Subagent can ask questions (before AND during work)\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"e3a633","wall_time_seconds":0.4003479,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":6344,"output":"---\\r\\nname: to-issues\\r\\ndescription: Break a plan, spec, or PRD into independently-grabbable issues on the project issue tracker using tracer-bullet vertical slices.\\r\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# To Issues\\r\\n\\r\\nBreak a plan into independently-grabbable issues using vertical slices (tracer bullets).\\r\\n\\r\\nThe issue tracker and triage label vocabulary should have been provided to you — run `/setup-matt-pocock-skills` if not.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Process\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Gather context\\r\\n\\r\\nWork from whatever is already in the conversation context. If the user passes an issue reference (issue number, URL, or path) as an argument, fetch it from the issue tracker and read its full body and comments.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Explore the codebase (optional)\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you have not already explored the codebase, do so to understand the current state of the code. Issue titles and descriptions should use the project\'s domain glossary vocabulary, and respect ADRs in the area you\'re touching.\\r\\n\\r\\nLook for opportunities to prefactor the code to make the implementation easier. \\"Make the change easy, then make the easy change.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Draft vertical slices\\r\\n\\r\\nBreak the plan into **tracer bullet** issues. Each issue is a thin vertical slice that cuts through ALL integration layers end-to-end, NOT a horizontal slice of one layer.\\r\\n\\r\\n<vertical-slice-rules>\\r\\n\\r\\n- Each slice delivers a narrow but COMPLETE path through every layer (schema, API, UI, tests)\\r\\n- A completed slice is demoable or verifiable on its own\\r\\n- Any prefactoring should be done first\\r\\n\\r\\n</vertical-slice-rules>\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4. Quiz the user\\r\\n\\r\\nPresent the proposed breakdown as a numbered list. For each slice, show:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Title**: short descriptive name\\r\\n- **Blocked by**: which other slices (if any) must complete first\\r\\n- **User stories covered**: which user stories this addresses (if the source material has them)\\r\\n\\r\\nAsk the user:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Does the granularity feel right? (too coarse / too fine)\\r\\n- Are the dependency relationships correct?\\r\\n- Should any slices be merged or split further?\\r\\n\\r\\nIterate until the user approves the breakdown.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 5. Publish the issues to the issue tracker\\r\\n\\r\\nFor each approved slice, publish a new issue to the issue tracker. Use the issue body template below. These issues are considered ready for AFK agents, so publish them with the correct triage label unless instructed otherwise.\\r\\n\\r\\nPublish issues in dependency order (blockers first) so you can reference real issue identifiers in the \\"Blocked by\\" field.\\r\\n\\r\\n<issue-template>\\r\\n## Parent\\r\\n\\r\\nA reference to the parent issue on the issue tracker (if the source was an existing issue, otherwise omit this section).\\r\\n\\r\\n## What to build\\r\\n\\r\\nA concise description of this vertical slice. Describe the end-to-end behavior, not layer-by-layer implementation.\\r\\n\\r\\nAvoid specific file paths or code snippets — they go stale fast. Exception: if a prototype produced a snippet that encodes a decision more precisely than prose can (state machine, reducer, schema, type shape), inline it here and note briefly that it came from a prototype. Trim to the decision-rich parts — not a working demo, just the important bits.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Acceptance criteria\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] Criterion 1\\r\\n- [ ] Criterion 2\\r\\n- [ ] Criterion 3\\r\\n\\r\\n## Blocked by\\r\\n\\r\\n- A reference to the blocking ticket (if any)\\r\\n\\r\\nOr \\"None - can start immediately\\" if no blockers.\\r\\n\\r\\n</issue-template>\\r\\n\\r\\nDo NOT close or modify any parent issue.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: to-spec\\r\\ndescription: \\"Turn the current conversation into a spec and publish it to the project issue tracker: no interview, just synthesis of what you\'ve already discussed.\\"\\r\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nThis skill takes the current conversation context and codebase understanding and produces a spec. Do NOT interview the user; just synthesize what you already know.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe issue tracker and triage label vocabulary should have been provided to you. If not, tell the user to run `/setup-matt-pocock-skills`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Process\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Explore the repo to understand the current state of the codebase, if you haven\'t already. Use the project\'s domain glossary vocabulary throughout the spec, and respect any ADRs in the area you\'re touching.\\r\\n\\r\\n2. Sketch out the seams at which you\'re going to test the feature. Existing seams should be preferred to new ones. Use the highest seam possible. If new seams are needed, propose them at the highest point you can. The fewer seams across the codebase, the better - the ideal number is one.\\r\\n\\r\\nCheck with the user that these seams match their expectations.\\r\\n\\r\\n3. Write the spec using the template below, then publish it to the project issue tracker. Apply the `ready-for-agent` triage label - no need for additional triage.\\r\\n\\r\\n<spec-template>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Problem Statement\\r\\n\\r\\nThe problem that the user is facing, from the user\'s perspective.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Solution\\r\\n\\r\\nThe solution to the problem, from the user\'s perspective.\\r\\n\\r\\n## User Stories\\r\\n\\r\\nA LONG, numbered list of user stories. Each user story should be in the format of:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. As an <actor>, I want a <feature>, so that <benefit>\\r\\n\\r\\n<user-story-example>\\r\\n1. As a mobile bank customer, I want to see balance on my accounts, so that I can make better informed decisions about my spending\\r\\n</user-story-example>\\r\\n\\r\\nThis list of user stories should be extremely extensive and cover all aspects of the feature.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Implementation Decisions\\r\\n\\r\\nA list of implementation decisions that were made. This can include:\\r\\n\\r\\n- The modules that will be built/modified\\r\\n- The interfaces of those modules that will be modified\\r\\n- Technical clarifications from the developer\\r\\n- Architectural decisions\\r\\n- Schema changes\\r\\n- API contracts\\r\\n- Specific interactions\\r\\n\\r\\nDo NOT include specific file paths or code snippets. They may end up being outdated very quickly.\\r\\n\\r\\nException: if a prototype produced a snippet that encodes a decision more precisely than prose can (state machine, reducer, schema, type shape), inline it within the relevant decision and note briefly that it came from a prototype. Trim to the decision-rich parts, not a working demo, just the important bits.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Testing Decisions\\r\\n\\r\\nA list of testing decisions that were made. Include:\\r\\n\\r\\n- A description of what makes a good test (only test external behavior, not implementation details)\\r\\n- Which modules will be tested\\r\\n- Prior art for the tests (i.e. similar types of tests in the codebase)\\r\\n\\r\\n## Out of Scope\\r\\n\\r\\nA description of the things that are out of scope for this spec.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Further Notes\\r\\n\\r\\nAny further notes about the feature.\\r\\n\\r\\n</spec-template>\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: to-tickets\\r\\ndescription: Break a plan, spec, or the current conversation into a set of tracer-bullet tickets, each declaring its blocking edges, published to the configured tracker (edges as text in one file per ticket locally, or native blocking links on a real tracker).\\r\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# To Tickets\\r\\n\\r\\nBreak a plan, spec, or conversation into a set of **tickets**: tracer-bullet vertical slices, each declaring the tickets that **block** it.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe issue tracker and triage label vocabulary should have been provided to you. If not, tell the user to run `/setup-matt-pocock-skills`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Process\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Gather context\\r\\n\\r\\nWork from whatever is already in the conversation context. If the user passes a reference (a spec path, an issue number or URL) as an argument, fetch it and read its full body and comments.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Explore the codebase (optional)\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you have not already explored the codebase, do so to understand the current state of the code. Ticket titles and descriptions should use the project\'s domain glossary vocabulary, and respect ADRs in the area you\'re touching.\\r\\n\\r\\nLook for opportunities to prefactor the code to make the implementation easier. \\"Make the change easy, then make the easy change.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Draft vertical slices\\r\\n\\r\\nBreak the work into **tracer bullet** tickets.\\r\\n\\r\\n<vertical-slice-rules>\\r\\n\\r\\n- Each slice cuts a narrow but COMPLETE path through every layer (schema, API, UI, tests): vertical, NOT a horizontal slice of one layer\\r\\n- A completed slice is demoable or verifiable on its own\\r\\n- Each slice is sized to fit in a single fresh context window\\r\\n- Any prefactoring should be done first\\r\\n\\r\\n</vertical-slice-rules>\\r\\n\\r\\nGive each ticket its **blocking edges**: the other tickets that must complete before it can start. A ticket with no blockers can start immediately.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Wide refactors are the exception to vertical slicing.** A **wide refactor** is one mechanical change (rename a column, retype a shared symbol) whose **blast radius** fans across the whole codebase, so a single edit breaks thousands of call sites at once and no vertical slice can land green. Don\'t force it into a tracer bullet; sequence it as **expand–contract**. First expand: add the new form beside the old so nothing breaks. Then migrate the call sites over in batches sized by blast radius (per package, per directory), each batch its own ticket blocked by the expand, keeping CI green batch to batch because the old form still exists. Finally contract: delete the old form once no caller remains, in a ticket blocked by every migrate batch. When even the batches can\'t stay green alone, keep the sequence but let them share an integration branch that all block a final integrate-and-verify ticket; green is promised only there.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4. Quiz the user\\r\\n\\r\\nPresent the proposed breakdown as a numbered list. For each ticket, show:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Title**: short descriptive name\\r\\n- **Blocked by**: which other tickets (if any) must complete first\\r\\n- **What it delivers**: the end-to-end behaviour this ticket makes work\\r\\n\\r\\nAsk the user:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Does the granularity feel right? (too coarse / too fine)\\r\\n- Are the blocking edges correct: does each ticket only depend on tickets that genuinely gate it?\\r\\n- Should any tickets be merged or split further?\\r\\n\\r\\nIterate until the user approves the breakdown.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 5. Publish the tickets to the configured tracker\\r\\n\\r\\nPublish the approved tickets. **How** depends on the tracker `/setup-matt-pocock-skills` configured; the tickets are the same either way, only the shape of the blocking edges changes:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Local files** → write one file per ticket under `.scratch/<feature-slug>/issues/<NN>-<slug>.md`, numbered from `01` in dependency order (blockers first). Each file\'s \\"Blocked by\\" lists the numbers/titles it depends on. Use the per-ticket file template below: one ticket per file, never a single combined file.\\r\\n- **A real issue tracker (GitHub, Linear, …)** → publish one issue per ticket in dependency order (blockers first) so each ticket\'s blocking edges can reference real identifiers. Use the platform\'s native blocking relationship where it has one; otherwise set each ticket\'s \\"Blocked by\\" to the blocking issues. If the source was an existing issue, make each ticket its sub-issue (tracker doc\'s operation). Apply the `ready-for-agent` triage label unless instructed otherwise; the tickets are agent-grabbable by construction.\\r\\n\\r\\nWork the **frontier**: any ticket whose blockers are all done. For a purely linear chain that means top to bottom.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo NOT close or modify any parent issue.\\r\\n\\r\\n<local-ticket-template>\\r\\n\\r\\n# <NN>: <Ticket title>\\r\\n\\r\\n**What to build:** the end-to-end behaviour this ticket makes work, from the user\'s perspective, not a layer-by-layer implementation list.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Blocked by:** the numbers/titles of the tickets that gate this one, or \\"None (can start immediately)\\".\\r\\n\\r\\n**Status:** ready-for-agent\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] Acceptance criterion 1\\r\\n- [ ] Acceptance criterion 2\\r\\n\\r\\n</local-ticket-template>\\r\\n\\r\\n<issue-template>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Parent\\r\\n\\r\\nA reference to the parent issue on the tracker (if the source was an existing issue, otherwise omit this section).\\r\\n\\r\\n## What to build\\r\\n\\r\\nThe end-to-end behaviour this ticket makes work, from the user\'s perspective, not layer-by-layer implementation.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Acceptance criteria\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] Criterion 1\\r\\n- [ ] Criterion 2\\r\\n\\r\\n## Blocked by\\r\\n\\r\\n- A reference to each blocking ticket, or \\"None (can start immediately)\\". Omit this section when blockers were set as native edges.\\r\\n\\r\\n</issue-template>\\r\\n\\r\\nIn either form, avoid specific file paths or code snippets: they go stale fast. Exception: if a prototype produced a snippet that encodes a decision more precisely than prose can (state machine, reducer, schema, type shape), inline it and note briefly that it came from a prototype. Trim to the decision-rich parts, not a working demo, just the important bits.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: troubleshooting\\r\\ndescription: Uses Chrome DevTools MCP and documentation to troubleshoot connection and target issues. Trigger this skill when list_pages, new_page, or navigate_page fail, or when the server initialization fails.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Troubleshooting Wizard\\r\\n\\r\\nYou are acting as a troubleshooting wizard to help the user configure and fix their Chrome DevTools MCP server setup. When this skill is triggered (e.g., because `list_pages`, `new_page`, or `navigate_page` failed, or the server wouldn\'t start), follow this step-by-step diagnostic process:\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 1: Find and Read Configuration\\r\\n\\r\\nYour first action should be to locate and read the MCP configuration file. Search for the following files in the user\'s workspace: `.mcp.json`, `gemini-extension.json`, `.claude/settings.json`, `.vscode/launch.json`, or `.gemini/settings.json`.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you find a configuration file, read and interpret it to identify potential issues such as:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Incorrect arguments or flags.\\r\\n- Missing environment variables.\\r\\n- Usage of `--autoConnect` in incompatible environments.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you cannot find any of these files, only then should you ask the user to provide their configuration file content.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 2: Triage Common Connection Errors\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore reading documentation or suggesting configuration changes, check if the error message matches one of the following common patterns.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Error: `Could not find DevToolsActivePort`\\r\\n\\r\\nThis error is highly specific to the `--autoConnect` feature. It means the MCP server cannot find the file created by a running, debuggable Chrome instance. This is not a generic connection failure.\\r\\n\\r\\nYour primary goal is to guide the user to ensure Chrome is running and properly configured. Do not immediately suggest switching to `--browserUrl`. Follow this exact sequence:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Ask the user to confirm that the correct Chrome version** (e.g., \\"Chrome Canary\\" if the error mentions it) is currently running.\\r\\n2. **If the user confirms it is running, instruct them to enable remote debugging.** Be very specific about the URL and the action: \\"Please open a new tab in Chrome, navigate to `chrome://inspect/#remote-debugging`, and make sure the \'Enable remote debugging\' checkbox is checked.\\"\\r\\n3. **Once the user confirms both steps, your only next action should be to call the `list_pages` tool.** This is the simplest and safest way to verify if the connection is now successful. Do not retry the original, more complex command yet.\\r\\n4. **If `list_pages` succeeds, the problem is resolved.** If it still fails with the same error, then you can proceed to the more advanced steps like suggesting `--browserUrl` or checking for sandboxing issues.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Symptom: Server starts but creates a new empty profile\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the server starts successfully but `list_pages` returns an empty list or creates a new profile instead of connecting to the existing Chrome instance, check for typos in the arguments.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Check for flag typos:** For example, `--autoBronnect` instead of `--autoConnect`.\\r\\n- **Verify the configuration:** Ensure the arguments match the expected flags exactly.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Other Common Errors\\r\\n\\r\\nIdentify other error messages from the failed tool call or the MCP initialization logs:\\r\\n\\r\\n- `Target closed`\\r\\n- \\"Tool not found\\" (check if they are using `--slim` which only enables navigation and screenshot tools).\\r\\n- `ProtocolError: Network.enable timed out` or `The socket connection was closed unexpectedly`\\r\\n- `Error [ERR_MODULE_NOT_FOUND]: Cannot find module`\\r\\n- Any sandboxing or host validation errors.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 3: Read Known Issues\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the contents of https://github.com/ChromeDevTools/chrome-devtools-mcp/blob/main/docs/troubleshooting.md to map the error to a known issue. Pay close attention to:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Sandboxing restrictions (macOS Seatbelt, Linux containers).\\r\\n- WSL requirements.\\r\\n- `--autoConnect` handshakes, timeouts, and requirements (requires **running** Chrome 144+).\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 4: Formulate a Configuration\\r\\n\\r\\nBased on the exact error and the user\'s environment (OS, MCP client), formulate the correct MCP configuration snippet. Check if they need to:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Pass `--browser-url=http://127.0.0.1:9222` instead of `--autoConnect` (e.g. if they are in a sandboxed environment like Claude Desktop).\\r\\n- Enable remote debugging in Chrome (`chrome://inspect/#remote-debugging`) and accept the connection prompt. **Ask the user to verify this is enabled if using `--autoConnect`.**\\r\\n- Add `--logFile <absolute_path_to_log_file>` to capture debug logs for analysis.\\r\\n- Increase `startup_timeout_ms` (e.g. to 20000) if using Codex on Windows.\\r\\n\\r\\n_If you are unsure of the user\'s configuration, ask the user to provide their current MCP server JSON configuration._\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 5: Run Diagnostic Commands\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the issue is still unclear, run diagnostic commands to test the server directly:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Run `npx chrome-devtools-mcp@latest --help` to verify the installation and Node.js environment.\\r\\n- If you need more information, run `DEBUG=* npx chrome-devtools-mcp@latest --logFile=/tmp/cdm-test.log` to capture verbose logs. Analyze the output for errors.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 6: Check GitHub for Existing Issues\\r\\n\\r\\nIf https://github.com/ChromeDevTools/chrome-devtools-mcp/blob/main/docs/troubleshooting.md does not cover the specific error, check if the `gh` (GitHub CLI) tool is available in the environment. If so, search the GitHub repository for similar issues:\\r\\n`gh issue list --repo ChromeDevTools/chrome-devtools-mcp --search \\"<error snippet>\\" --state all`\\r\\n\\r\\nAlternatively, you can recommend that the user checks https://github.com/ChromeDevTools/chrome-devtools-mcp/issues and https://github.com/ChromeDevTools/chrome-devtools-mcp/discussions for help.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: tutorocean-profile-fill\\r\\ndescription: Fill and verify Joshua\'s TutorOcean tutor profile using vault facts, then stop before uploads, policy agreement, or final submission unless the user explicitly authorizes those actions.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# TutorOcean Profile Fill\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this skill when working in TutorOcean\'s tutor application or profile flow for Joshua.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Read first\\r\\n\\r\\n1. `wiki/knowledge/tutoring-outreach-log.md`\\r\\n2. `wiki/knowledge/tutoring-profile-facts.md`\\r\\n3. `wiki/knowledge/tutoring-form-fill-instructions.md`\\r\\n\\r\\n## Working rules\\r\\n\\r\\n- Use only confirmed facts from the vault or the user.\\r\\n- Prefer structured, patient LSAT coaching language.\\r\\n- Do not invent education dates, credentials, uploads, or years of experience.\\r\\n- Treat uploads and submission as separate actions that need explicit user confirmation.\\r\\n- If a save fails, retry once after verifying the field values. If it fails again, stop and report the platform error.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Defaults\\r\\n\\r\\n- Email: `contact@aspiringattorneys.com`\\r\\n- Phone: `613 276 2604`\\r\\n- Location: `Ottawa, Ontario, Canada`\\r\\n- Street address: `96 Woodfield Drive`\\r\\n- Postal code: `K2G 0A3`\\r\\n- Gender: `Male`\\r\\n- Rate: `40 USD`\\r\\n- Subject: `LSAT`\\r\\n- Levels: `College/University`, `Professional`\\r\\n- Work experience: `Aspiring Attorneys`, `Founder and CEO`, `May 2018` to `present`\\r\\n\\r\\n## Profile copy\\r\\n\\r\\nUse the LSAT story already saved in the vault:\\r\\n\\r\\n- `138 -> 173`\\r\\n- the LSAT did not come naturally\\r\\n- structured and intuitive approach\\r\\n- teaching for a few years\\r\\n- helps with stuck-between-two-answers, timing, and inconsistent-plan issues\\r\\n- LSAT U practice space when the field supports it\\r\\n\\r\\n## Execution order\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Fill the confirmed personal information fields.\\r\\n2. Fill subject, level, rate, and location fields.\\r\\n3. Fill the bio and verification text with the saved vault copy.\\r\\n4. Add work experience with the resume-backed facts.\\r\\n5. Verify the profile state after each save.\\r\\n6. Stop before education uploads, ID uploads, policy agreement, or application submission unless the user explicitly says to continue.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Verification standard\\r\\n\\r\\nAfter saving, check the page again and confirm the persisted values in the profile view, not just the modal state.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: ubiquitous-language\\r\\ndescription: Extract a DDD-style ubiquitous language glossary from the current conversation, flagging ambiguities and proposing canonical terms. Saves to UBIQUITOUS_LANGUAGE.md. Use when user wants to define domain terms, build a glossary, harden terminology, create a ubiquitous language, or mentions \\"domain model\\" or \\"DDD\\".\\r\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Ubiquitous Language\\r\\n\\r\\nExtract and formalize domain terminology from the current conversation into a consistent glossary, saved to a local file.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Process\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Scan the conversation** for domain-relevant nouns, verbs, and concepts\\r\\n2. **Identify problems**:\\r\\n   - Same word used for different concepts (ambiguity)\\r\\n   - Different words used for the same concept (synonyms)\\r\\n   - Vague or overloaded terms\\r\\n3. **Propose a canonical glossary** with opinionated term choices\\r\\n4. **Write to `UBIQUITOUS_LANGUAGE.md`** in the working directory using the format below\\r\\n5. **Output a summary** inline in the conversation\\r\\n\\r\\n## Output Format\\r\\n\\r\\nWrite a `UBIQUITOUS_LANGUAGE.md` file with this structure:\\r\\n\\r\\n```md\\r\\n# Ubiquitous Language\\r\\n\\r\\n## Order lifecycle\\r\\n\\r\\n| Term        | Definition                                              | Aliases to avoid      |\\r\\n| ----------- | ------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------- |\\r\\n| **Order**   | A customer\'s request to purchase one or more items      | Purchase, transaction |\\r\\n| **Invoice** | A request for payment sent to a customer after delivery | Bill, payment request |\\r\\n\\r\\n## People\\r\\n\\r\\n| Term         | Definition                                  | Aliases to avoid       |\\r\\n| ------------ | ------------------------------------------- | ---------------------- |\\r\\n| **Customer** | A person or organization that places orders | Client, buyer, account |\\r\\n| **User**     | An authentication identity in the system    | Login, account         |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Relationships\\r\\n\\r\\n- An **Invoice** belongs to exactly one **Customer**\\r\\n- An **Order** produces one or more **Invoices**\\r\\n\\r\\n## Example dialogue\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Dev:** \\"When a **Customer** places an **Order**, do we create the **Invoice** immediately?\\"\\r\\n> **Domain expert:** \\"No — an **Invoice** is only generated once a **Fulfillment** is confirmed. A single **Order** can produce multiple **Invoices** if items ship in separate **Shipments**.\\"\\r\\n> **Dev:** \\"So if a **Shipment** is cancelled before dispatch, no **Invoice** exists for it?\\"\\r\\n> **Domain expert:** \\"Exactly. The **Invoice** lifecycle is tied to the **Fulfillment**, not the **Order**.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n## Flagged ambiguities\\r\\n\\r\\n- \\"account\\" was used to mean both **Customer** and **User** — these are distinct concepts: a **Customer** places orders, while a **User** is an authentication identity that may or may not represent a **Customer**.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Rules\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Be opinionated.** When multiple words exist for the same concept, pick the best one and list the others as aliases to avoid.\\r\\n- **Flag conflicts explicitly.** If a term is used ambiguously in the conversation, call it out in the \\"Flagged ambiguities\\" section with a clear recommendation.\\r\\n- **Only include terms relevant for domain experts.** Skip the names of modules or classes unless they have meaning in the domain language.\\r\\n- **Keep definitions tight.** One sentence max. Define what it IS, not what it does.\\r\\n- **Show relationships.** Use bold term names and express cardinality where obvious.\\r\\n- **Only include domain terms.** Skip generic programming concepts (array, function, endpoint) unless they have domain-specific meaning.\\r\\n- **Group terms into multiple tables** when natural clusters emerge (e.g. by subdomain, lifecycle, or actor). Each group gets its own heading and table. If all terms belong to a single cohesive domain, one table is fine — don\'t force groupings.\\r\\n- **Write an example dialogue.** A short conversation (3-5 exchanges) between a dev and a domain expert that demonstrates how the terms interact naturally. The dialogue should clarify boundaries between related concepts and show terms being used precisely.\\r\\n\\r\\n<example>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Example dialogue\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Dev:** \\"How do I test the **sync service** without Docker?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Domain expert:** \\"Provide the **filesystem layer** instead of the **Docker layer**. It implements the same **Sandbox service** interface but uses a local directory as the **sandbox**.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Dev:** \\"So **sync-in** still creates a **bundle** and unpacks it?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n> **Domain expert:** \\"Exactly. The **sync service** doesn\'t know which layer it\'s talking to. It calls `exec` and `copyIn` — the **filesystem layer** just runs those as local shell commands.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n</example>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Re-running\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen invoked again in the same conversation:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Read the existing `UBIQUITOUS_LANGUAGE.md`\\r\\n2. Incorporate any new terms from subsequent discussion\\r\\n3. Update definitions if understanding has evolved\\r\\n4. Re-flag any new ambiguities\\r\\n5. Rewrite the example dialogue to incorporate new terms\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"Get-Content skills/claude/verification-before-completion/SKILL.md, skills/claude/wayfinder/SKILL.md, skills/claude/web-artifacts-builder/SKILL.md, skills/claude/webapp-testing/SKILL.md, skills/claude/wizard/SKILL.md",max_output_tokens:6500}));


## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 4.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"e8edac","wall_time_seconds":0.4150146,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":7047,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 7047)\\nTotal output lines: 481\\n\\n---\\r\\nname: verification-before-completion\\r\\ndescription: Use when about to claim work is complete, fixed, or passing, before committing or creating PRs - requires running verification commands and confirming output before making any success claims; evidence before assertions always\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Verification Before Completion\\r\\n\\r\\n## Overview\\r\\n\\r\\nClaiming work is complete without verification is dishonesty, not efficiency.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Core principle:** Evidence before claims, always.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Violating the letter of this rule is violating the spirit of this rule.**\\r\\n\\r\\n## The Iron Law\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nNO COMPLETION CLAIMS WITHOUT FRESH VERIFICATION EVIDENCE\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you haven\'t run the verification command in this message, you cannot claim it passes.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The Gate Function\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nBEFORE claiming any status or expressing satisfaction:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. IDENTIFY: What command proves this claim?\\r\\n2. RUN: Execute the FULL command (fresh, complete)\\r\\n3. READ: Full output, check exit code, count failures\\r\\n4. VERIFY: Does output confirm the claim?\\r\\n   - If NO: State actual status with evidence\\r\\n   - If YES: State claim WITH evidence\\r\\n5. ONLY THEN: Make the claim\\r\\n\\r\\nSkip any step = lying, not verifying\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Common Failures\\r\\n\\r\\n| Claim | Requires | Not Sufficient |\\r\\n|-------|----------|----------------|\\r\\n| Tests pass | Test command output: 0 failures | Previous run, \\"should pass\\" |\\r\\n| Linter clean | Linter output: 0 errors | Partial check, extrapolation |\\r\\n| Build succeeds | Build command: exit 0 | Linter passing, logs look good |\\r\\n| Bug fixed | Test original symptom: passes | Code changed, assumed fixed |\\r\\n| Regression test works | Red-green cycle verified | Test passes once |\\r\\n| Agent completed | VCS diff shows changes | Agent reports \\"success\\" |\\r\\n| Requirements met | Line-by-line checklist | Tests passing |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Red Flags - STOP\\r\\n\\r\\n- Using \\"should\\", \\"probably\\", \\"seems to\\"\\r\\n- Expressing satisfaction before verification (\\"Great!\\", \\"Perfect!\\", \\"Done!\\", etc.)\\r\\n- About to commit/push/PR without verification\\r\\n- Trusting agent success reports\\r\\n- Relying on partial verification\\r\\n- Thinking \\"just this once\\"\\r\\n- Tired and wanting work over\\r\\n- **ANY wording implying success without having run verification**\\r\\n\\r\\n## Rationalization Prevention\\r\\n\\r\\n| Excuse | Reality |\\r\\n|--------|---------|\\r\\n| \\"Should work now\\" | RUN the verification |\\r\\n| \\"I\'m confident\\" | Confidence ≠ evidence |\\r\\n| \\"Just this once\\" | No exceptions |\\r\\n| \\"Linter passed\\" | Linter ≠ compiler |\\r\\n| \\"Agent said success\\" | Verify independently |\\r\\n| \\"I\'m tired\\" | Exhaustion ≠ excuse |\\r\\n| \\"Partial check is enough\\" | Partial proves nothing |\\r\\n| \\"Different words so rule doesn\'t apply\\" | Spirit over letter |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Key Patterns\\r\\n\\r\\n**Tests:**\\r\\n```\\r\\n✅ [Run test command] [See: 34/34 pass] \\"All tests pass\\"\\r\\n❌ \\"Should pass now\\" / \\"Looks correct\\"\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**Regression tests (TDD Red-Green):**\\r\\n```\\r\\n✅ Write → Run (pass) → Revert fix → Run (MUST FAIL) → Restore → Run (pass)\\r\\n❌ \\"I\'ve written a regression test\\" (without red-green verification)\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**Build:**\\r\\n```\\r\\n✅ [Run build] [See: exit 0] \\"Build passes\\"\\r\\n❌ \\"Linter passed\\" (linter doesn\'t check compilation)\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**Requirements:**\\r\\n```\\r\\n✅ Re-read plan → Create checklist → Verify each → Report gaps or completion\\r\\n❌ \\"Tests pass, phase complete\\"\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**Agent delegation:**\\r\\n```\\r\\n✅ Agent reports success → Check VCS diff → Verify changes → Report actual state\\r\\n❌ Trust agent report\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Why This Matters\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom 24 failure memories:\\r\\n- your human partner said \\"I don\'t believe you\\" - trust broken\\r\\n- Undefined functions shipped - would crash\\r\\n- Missing requirements shipped - incomplete features\\r\\n- Time wasted on false completion → redirect → rework\\r\\n- Violates: \\"Honesty is a core value. If you lie, you\'ll be replaced.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n## When To Apply\\r\\n\\r\\n**ALWAYS before:**\\r\\n- ANY variation of success/completion claims\\r\\n- ANY expression of satisfaction\\r\\n- ANY positive statement about work state\\r\\n- Committing, PR creation, task completion\\r\\n- Moving to next task\\r\\n- Delegating to agents\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule applies to:**\\r\\n- Exact phrases\\r\\n- Paraphrases and synonyms\\r\\n- Implications of success\\r\\n- ANY communication suggesting completion/correctness\\r\\n\\r\\n## The Bottom Line\\r\\n\\r\\n**No shortcuts for verification.**\\r\\n\\r\\nRun the command. Read the output. THEN claim the result.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is non-negotiable.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: wayfinder\\r\\ndescription: Plan a huge chunk of work (more than one agent session can hold) as a shared map of decision tickets on your issue tracker, and resolve them one at a time until the way to the destination is clear.\\r\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nA loose idea has arrived, too big for one agent session, and wrapped in fog: the way from here to the **destination** isn\'t visible yet. Wayfinding is about finding that way, not charging at the destination. This skill charts the way as a **shared map** on the repo\'s issue tracker, then works its **decision tickets** (questions whose resolution is a decision, not slices of a build to execute) one at a time until the route is clear.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe destination varies per effort, and naming it is the first act of charting: it shapes every ticket. It might be a spec to hand off and iterate on, a decision to lock before planning starts, or a change made in place like a data-structure migration. The map is domain-agnostic: engineering work, course content, whatever fits the shape.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Plan, don\'t do\\r\\n\\r\\nWayfinder is **planning** by default: each ticket resolves a decision, and the map is done when the way is clear, with nothing left to decide before someone goes and does the thing. The pull to just do the work is usually the signal you\'ve reached the edge of the map and it\'s time to hand off. An effort can override this in its **Notes**, carrying execution into the map itself, but absent that, produce decisions, not deliverables.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Refer by name\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery map and ticket is an issue, so it has a **name**: its title. In everything the human reads (narration, the map\'s Decisions-so-far), refer to it by that name, never by a bare id, number, or slug. A wall of `#42, #43, #44` is illegible; names read at a glance. The id and URL don\'t vanish; a name wraps its link, but they ride _inside_ the name, never stand in for it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The Map\\r\\n\\r\\nThe map is a single issue on this repo\'s issue tracker, labelled `wayfinder:map`, the canonical artifact. Its tickets are child issues of the map.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe map is an **index**, not a store. It lists the decisions made and points at the tickets that hold their detail; a decision lives in exactly one place, its ticket, so the map never restates it, only gists it and links.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Where the map, its child tickets, blocking, and frontier queries physically live is tracker-specific.** The issue tracker should have been provided to you. If not, tell the user to run `/setup-matt-pocock-skills`. Consult the tracker doc\'s \\"Wayfinding operations\\" section for how _this_ repo expresses them. If no tracker has been provided, default to the local-markdown tracker.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The map body\\r\\n\\r\\nThe whole map at low resolution, loaded once per session. Open tickets are **not** listed: they are open child issues, found by query.\\r\\n\\r\\n```markdown\\r\\n## Destination\\r\\n\\r\\n<what reaching the end of this map looks like: the spec, decision, or change this effort is finding its way to. One or two lines; every session orients to it before choosing a ticket.>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Notes\\r\\n\\r\\n<domain; skills every session should consult; standing preferences for this effort>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Decisions so far\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- the index: one line per closed ticket, enough to judge relevance, then zoom the link for the detail the ticket holds -->\\r\\n\\r\\n- [<closed ticket title>](link): <one-line gist of the answer>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Not yet specified\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- see \\"Fog of war\\": in-scope fog you can\'t ticket yet; graduates as the frontier advances -->\\r\\n\\r\\n## Out of scope\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- see \\"Out of scope\\": work ruled beyond the destination; closed, never graduates -->\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### Tickets\\r\\n\\r\\nEach ticket is a **child issue** of the map; the tracker\'s issue id is its identity. Its body is the question, sized to one 100K token agent session:\\r\\n\\r\\n```markdown\\r\\n## Question\\r\\n\\r\\n<the decision or investigation this ticket resolves>\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nEach ticket carries a `wayfinder:<type>` label, one of `research`, `prototype`, `grilling`, `task` (see [Ticket Types](#ticket-types)). `wayfinder:` labels are the only labels a map and its tickets carry, never a triage label like `ready-for-agent`: they are decisions, not implementation work.\\r\\n\\r\\nA session **claims** a ticket by assigning it to the dev driving the map, **first**, before any work, so concurrent sessions skip it. That assignee _is_ the claim: an open, unassigned ticket is unclaimed.\\r\\n\\r\\nBlocking uses the tracker\'s **native** dependency relationship: essential because it renders the frontier _visually_ in the tracker\'s own UI, so the human sees what\'s takeable without opening the map. Only a tracker that lacks native blocking falls back to a body convention. A ticket is **unblocked** when every ticket blocking it is closed; the **frontier** is the open, unblocked, unclaimed children, the edge of the known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe answer isn\'t part of the body; it\'s recorded on resolution (see [Work through the map](#work-through-the-map)). Assets created while resolving a ticket are linked from the issue, not pasted in.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Ticket Types\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery ticket is either **HITL** (human in the loop, worked _with_ a human who speaks for themselves) or **AFK**, driven by the agent alone. A HITL ticket only resolves through that live exchange; the agent never stands in for the human\'s side of it (a grilling agent that answers its own questions has broken this).\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Research** (AFK): Reading documentation, third-party APIs, or local resources like knowledge bases to surface a fact a decision waits on. Resolved by a subagent that calls the Skill tool with \\"research\\". Use when knowledge outside the current working directory is required.\\r\\n- **Prototype** (HITL): Raise the fidelity of the discussion by making a cheap, rough, concrete artifact to react to (an outline, a rough take, a stub, or UI/logic code) by calling the Skill tool with \\"prototype\\". Links the prototype as an asset. Use when \\"how should it look\\" or \\"how should it behave\\" is the key question.\\r\\n- **Grilling** (HITL): Conversation. The default case. Always call the Skill tool twice, for \\"grilling\\" and \\"domain-modeling\\".\\r\\n- **Task** (HITL or AFK): Manual work that must happen before a _decision_ can be made: nothing to decide, prototype, or research, but the discussion is blocked until it\'s done. Signing up for a service so its API can be judged, provisioning access, moving data so its shape can be seen. This is the one type that _does_ rather than decides, and it earns its place by unblocking a decision, not by delivering the destination. The agent drives it alone where it can (AFK); otherwise it hands the human a precise checklist (HITL). Resolved when the work is done; the answer records what was done and any resulting facts (credentials location, new URLs, row counts) later tickets depend on.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Fog of war\\r\\n\\r\\nThe map is _deliberately_ incomplete: don\'t chart what you can\'t yet see. Beyond the live tickets lies the **fog of war**: the dim view of decisions and investigations you can tell are coming but can\'t yet pin down, because they hang on questions still open. Resolving a ticket clears the fog ahead of it, graduating whatever\'s now specifiable into fresh tickets, one at a time, until the way to the destination is clear and no tickets remain.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe map\'s **Not yet specified** section is where that dim view is written down: the suspected question, the area to revisit later. It\'s the undiscovered frontier _toward_ the destination: everything here is in scope, just not sharp enough to ticket. Write as loosely or as fully as the view allows; it doubles as a signpost for collaborators reading where the effort is headed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Fog or ticket?** The test is whether you can state the question precisely now, _not_ whether you can answer it now.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Ticket when** the question is already sharp, even if it\'s blocked and you can\'t act on it yet.\\r\\n- **Not yet specified when** you can\'t yet phrase it that sharply. Don\'t pre-slice the fog into ticket-sized pieces: it\'s coarser than a ticket, and one patch may graduate into several tickets, or none, once the frontier reaches it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Not yet specified** excludes what\'s already decided (Decisions so far), what\'s already a live ticket, and what\'s out of scope (the next section).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Out of scope\\r\\n\\r\\nFog only ever gathers _toward_ the destination. The destination fixes the scope, so work beyond it is **out of scope**: it isn\'t fog, and it doesn\'t belong in **Not yet specified**. It gets its own **Out of scope** section on the map: work you\'ve consciously ruled out of _this_ effort. Scope, not sharpness, lands it here.\\r\\n\\r\\nOut-of-scope work never graduates (the frontier stops at the destination), so it returns o…547 tokens truncated… `research/<name>` branch with a context pointer from the ticket. Push the branch but open no PR: it is never merged.\\r\\n6. Stop: charting is one session\'s work; it hand-resolves nothing.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Work through the map\\r\\n\\r\\nUser invokes with a map (URL or number). A ticket is **optional**: without one, you pick the next decision, not the user.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Load the **map**: the low-res view, not every ticket body.\\r\\n2. Choose the ticket. If the user named one, use it. Otherwise take the first frontier ticket in order. **Claim it**: assign it to yourself before any work.\\r\\n3. Resolve it as the type its `wayfinder:<type>` label names (see [Ticket Types](#ticket-types)). Read the label, not just the body: the body never states the type. **Zoom as needed**: fetch the full body of any related or closed ticket on demand; call the Skill tool for whichever skills the `## Notes` block names. If in doubt, call the Skill tool twice, for \\"grilling\\" and \\"domain-modeling\\".\\r\\n4. Record the resolution: post the answer as a **resolution comment**, **close** the issue, and **append a context pointer** to the map\'s Decisions-so-far.\\r\\n5. Add newly-surfaced tickets (create-then-wire); graduate any fog the answer has made specifiable, clearing each graduated patch from **Not yet specified** so it lives only as its new ticket. If the answer reveals that a ticket (this one or another) sits beyond the destination, **rule it out of scope** rather than resolving it on the route. If the decision invalidates other parts of the map, update or delete those tickets.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe user may run unblocked tickets in parallel, so expect other sessions to be editing the tracker concurrently.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: web-artifacts-builder\\r\\ndescription: Suite of tools for creating elaborate, multi-component claude.ai HTML artifacts using modern frontend web technologies (React, Tailwind CSS, shadcn/ui). Use for complex artifacts requiring state management, routing, or shadcn/ui components - not for simple single-file HTML/JSX artifacts.\\r\\nlicense: Complete terms in LICENSE.txt\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Web Artifacts Builder\\r\\n\\r\\nTo build powerful frontend claude.ai artifacts, follow these steps:\\r\\n1. Initialize the frontend repo using `scripts/init-artifact.sh`\\r\\n2. Develop your artifact by editing the generated code\\r\\n3. Bundle all code into a single HTML file using `scripts/bundle-artifact.sh`\\r\\n4. Display artifact to user\\r\\n5. (Optional) Test the artifact\\r\\n\\r\\n**Stack**: React 18 + TypeScript + Vite + Parcel (bundling) + Tailwind CSS + shadcn/ui\\r\\n\\r\\n## Design & Style Guidelines\\r\\n\\r\\nVERY IMPORTANT: To avoid what is often referred to as \\"AI slop\\", avoid using excessive centered layouts, purple gradients, uniform rounded corners, and Inter font.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Quick Start\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 1: Initialize Project\\r\\n\\r\\nRun the initialization script to create a new React project:\\r\\n```bash\\r\\nbash scripts/init-artifact.sh <project-name>\\r\\ncd <project-name>\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThis creates a fully configured project with:\\r\\n- ✅ React + TypeScript (via Vite)\\r\\n- ✅ Tailwind CSS 3.4.1 with shadcn/ui theming system\\r\\n- ✅ Path aliases (`@/`) configured\\r\\n- ✅ 40+ shadcn/ui components pre-installed\\r\\n- ✅ All Radix UI dependencies included\\r\\n- ✅ Parcel configured for bundling (via .parcelrc)\\r\\n- ✅ Node 18+ compatibility (auto-detects and pins Vite version)\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 2: Develop Your Artifact\\r\\n\\r\\nTo build the artifact, edit the generated files. See **Common Development Tasks** below for guidance.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 3: Bundle to Single HTML File\\r\\n\\r\\nTo bundle the React app into a single HTML artifact:\\r\\n```bash\\r\\nbash scripts/bundle-artifact.sh\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThis creates `bundle.html` - a self-contained artifact with all JavaScript, CSS, and dependencies inlined. This file can be directly shared in Claude conversations as an artifact.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Requirements**: Your project must have an `index.html` in the root directory.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What the script does**:\\r\\n- Installs bundling dependencies (parcel, @parcel/config-default, parcel-resolver-tspaths, html-inline)\\r\\n- Creates `.parcelrc` config with path alias support\\r\\n- Builds with Parcel (no source maps)\\r\\n- Inlines all assets into single HTML using html-inline\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 4: Share Artifact with User\\r\\n\\r\\nFinally, share the bundled HTML file in conversation with the user so they can view it as an artifact.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 5: Testing/Visualizing the Artifact (Optional)\\r\\n\\r\\nNote: This is a completely optional step. Only perform if necessary or requested.\\r\\n\\r\\nTo test/visualize the artifact, use available tools (including other Skills or built-in tools like Playwright or Puppeteer). In general, avoid testing the artifact upfront as it adds latency between the request and when the finished artifact can be seen. Test later, after presenting the artifact, if requested or if issues arise.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Reference\\r\\n\\r\\n- **shadcn/ui components**: https://ui.shadcn.com/docs/components\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: webapp-testing\\r\\ndescription: Toolkit for interacting with and testing local web applications using Playwright. Supports verifying frontend functionality, debugging UI behavior, capturing browser screenshots, and viewing browser logs.\\r\\nlicense: Complete terms in LICENSE.txt\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Web Application Testing\\r\\n\\r\\nTo test local web applications, write native Python Playwright scripts.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Helper Scripts Available**:\\r\\n- `scripts/with_server.py` - Manages server lifecycle (supports multiple servers)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Always run scripts with `--help` first** to see usage. DO NOT read the source until you try running the script first and find that a customized solution is abslutely necessary. These scripts can be very large and thus pollute your context window. They exist to be called directly as black-box scripts rather than ingested into your context window.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Decision Tree: Choosing Your Approach\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nUser task → Is it static HTML?\\r\\n    ├─ Yes → Read HTML file directly to identify selectors\\r\\n    │         ├─ Success → Write Playwright script using selectors\\r\\n    │         └─ Fails/Incomplete → Treat as dynamic (below)\\r\\n    │\\r\\n    └─ No (dynamic webapp) → Is the server already running?\\r\\n        ├─ No → Run: python scripts/with_server.py --help\\r\\n        │        Then use the helper + write simplified Playwright script\\r\\n        │\\r\\n        └─ Yes → Reconnaissance-then-action:\\r\\n            1. Navigate and wait for networkidle\\r\\n            2. Take screenshot or inspect DOM\\r\\n            3. Identify selectors from rendered state\\r\\n            4. Execute actions with discovered selectors\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Example: Using with_server.py\\r\\n\\r\\nTo start a server, run `--help` first, then use the helper:\\r\\n\\r\\n**Single server:**\\r\\n```bash\\r\\npython scripts/with_server.py --server \\"npm run dev\\" --port 5173 -- python your_automation.py\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n**Multiple servers (e.g., backend + frontend):**\\r\\n```bash\\r\\npython scripts/with_server.py \\\\\\r\\n  --server \\"cd backend && python server.py\\" --port 3000 \\\\\\r\\n  --server \\"cd frontend && npm run dev\\" --port 5173 \\\\\\r\\n  -- python your_automation.py\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nTo create an automation script, include only Playwright logic (servers are managed automatically):\\r\\n```python\\r\\nfrom playwright.sync_api import sync_playwright\\r\\n\\r\\nwith sync_playwright() as p:\\r\\n    browser = p.chromium.launch(headless=True) # Always launch chromium in headless mode\\r\\n    page = browser.new_page()\\r\\n    page.goto(\'http://localhost:5173\') # Server already running and ready\\r\\n    page.wait_for_load_state(\'networkidle\') # CRITICAL: Wait for JS to execute\\r\\n    # ... your automation logic\\r\\n    browser.close()\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Reconnaissance-Then-Action Pattern\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Inspect rendered DOM**:\\r\\n   ```python\\r\\n   page.screenshot(path=\'/tmp/inspect.png\', full_page=True)\\r\\n   content = page.content()\\r\\n   page.locator(\'button\').all()\\r\\n   ```\\r\\n\\r\\n2. **Identify selectors** from inspection results\\r\\n\\r\\n3. **Execute actions** using discovered selectors\\r\\n\\r\\n## Common Pitfall\\r\\n\\r\\n❌ **Don\'t** inspect the DOM before waiting for `networkidle` on dynamic apps\\r\\n✅ **Do** wait for `page.wait_for_load_state(\'networkidle\')` before inspection\\r\\n\\r\\n## Best Practices\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Use bundled scripts as black boxes** - To accomplish a task, consider whether one of the scripts available in `scripts/` can help. These scripts handle common, complex workflows reliably without cluttering the context window. Use `--help` to see usage, then invoke directly. \\r\\n- Use `sync_playwright()` for synchronous scripts\\r\\n- Always close the browser when done\\r\\n- Use descriptive selectors: `text=`, `role=`, CSS selectors, or IDs\\r\\n- Add appropriate waits: `page.wait_for_selector()` or `page.wait_for_timeout()`\\r\\n\\r\\n## Reference Files\\r\\n\\r\\n- **examples/** - Examples showing common patterns:\\r\\n  - `element_discovery.py` - Discovering buttons, links, and inputs on a page\\r\\n  - `static_html_automation.py` - Using file:// URLs for local HTML\\r\\n  - `console_logging.py` - Capturing console logs during automation\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: wizard\\r\\ndescription: Generate an interactive bash wizard that walks a human through steps only they can perform. Use when provisioning infrastructure, setting up credentials or CI secrets, walking an unfamiliar third-party dashboard, or running a one-off migration or cutover. Don\'t invoke this for steps the agent can perform itself.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Wizard\\r\\n\\r\\nA **wizard** is a bash script that walks a human, step by step, through a manual procedure that\'s tedious to do by hand and tedious to re-explain to an AI every time. It opens each URL, says exactly what to click and copy, captures the values, writes them where they belong (`.env`, GitHub secrets), confirms at every stage, and shows how many stages are left. It might configure third-party services, run a one-off migration, or move the project from one state to another.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe delightful UX is already solved by [template.sh](template.sh): stage-by-stage progress, confirmation gates, cross-platform URL opening (including WSL), hidden secret entry, idempotent `.env` upserts, `gh secret`/`gh variable` writes, and a closing summary. **Your job is only to scope the procedure and author its stages.** The library above the `STAGES` marker is identical in every wizard; that consistency is the point: never hand-edit it.\\r\\n\\r\\nA wizard is ephemeral by default: built for one run, saved to a scratch or `scripts/` path, deleted when the job\'s done. Commit it only when the user wants a repeatable setup path that should live in the repo.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Process\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Scope the procedure\\r\\n\\r\\nWork out every manual step the human must take and every value that gets captured along the way. Read the repo first, don\'t ask cold:\\r\\n\\r\\n- For setup: `.env`, `.env.example`, `.env.*`, `README`, `docker-compose*`, framework config, and `.github/workflows/*` (every `secrets.*` / `vars.*` reference is a value the wizard must produce).\\r\\n- For a migration or transition: the current state, the target state, and the irreversible actions between them.\\r\\n\\r\\nThen show the user the ordered list of stages and the values each produces, and confirm: they may add, drop, or reorder.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Done when:** every stage is named in order, and for each captured value you know (a) where the human gets it, (b) where it\'s written (`.env`, a GitHub secret, both, or nowhere; some stages are pure actions), and (c) whether it\'s secret (hidden entry) or public.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Map each stage\'s journey\\r\\n\\r\\nFor each stage, write the precise path a human follows: which URL to open, what to do there, where a value is shown, which variable it fills: e.g. \\"Dashboard → Developers → API keys → Reveal test key → copy\\". Where you don\'t actually know the current UI or the exact command, say so and ask the user or check the docs: never invent steps that may not exist.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Done when:** every stage traces to concrete instructions a stranger could follow.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Author the wizard\\r\\n\\r\\nCopy `template.sh` to the target path. Replace the example stage with one `stage` per step, in dependency order. Use the library helpers: `stage`, `say`/`step`, `open_url`, `ask`/`ask_secret`, `write_env`, `set_secret`/`set_var`, `pause`/`confirm`. Set `TOTAL_STAGES` to the number of stages you wrote.\\r\\n\\r\\nHold the bar the template sets: open the URL before asking for its value, use `ask_secret` for anything secret, `write_env` every persisted value, `set_secret` only the values CI actually needs, and `confirm` before any irreversible action. Each `stage` clears the screen so only the current step is visible: keep a stage to one focused task so nothing the human needs scrolls away. Don\'t touch the library above the marker.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4. Verify and hand off\\r\\n\\r\\n- `bash -n <script>`; run `shellcheck` if available.\\r\\n- `chmod +x <script>`.\\r\\n- Don\'t run it end-to-end yourself: it opens browsers and blocks on human input. Trace it statically instead: every value from step 1 is captured and lands where step 1 said, and every `set_secret` name exactly matches a `secrets.*` reference in CI.\\r\\n- Tell the user how to run it. If it\'s a repeatable setup path, commit it and link it from the README so the next person runs the script instead of asking an AI.\\r\\n"}'}]
