## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 8.1 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"f1b90a","wall_time_seconds":0.3478232,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":933,"output":"The map\'s **Not yet specified** section is where that dim view is written down: the suspected question, the area to revisit later. It\'s the undiscovered frontier _toward_ the destination: everything here is in scope, just not sharp enough to ticket. Write as loosely or as fully as the view allows; it doubles as a signpost for collaborators reading where the effort is headed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Fog or ticket?** The test is whether you can state the question precisely now, _not_ whether you can answer it now.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Ticket when** the question is already sharp, even if it\'s blocked and you can\'t act on it yet.\\r\\n- **Not yet specified when** you can\'t yet phrase it that sharply. Don\'t pre-slice the fog into ticket-sized pieces: it\'s coarser than a ticket, and one patch may graduate into several tickets, or none, once the frontier reaches it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Not yet specified** excludes what\'s already decided (Decisions so far), what\'s already a live ticket, and what\'s out of scope (the next section).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Out of scope\\r\\n\\r\\nFog only ever gathers _toward_ the destination. The destination fixes the scope, so work beyond it is **out of scope**: it isn\'t fog, and it doesn\'t belong in **Not yet specified**. It gets its own **Out of scope** section on the map: work you\'ve consciously ruled out of _this_ effort. Scope, not sharpness, lands it here.\\r\\n\\r\\nOut-of-scope work never graduates (the frontier stops at the destination), so it returns only if the destination is redrawn, and then as a fresh effort, not a resumption.\\r\\n\\r\\nRuling something out of scope is a scoping act, not a step on the route. When a ticket that already exists turns out to sit past the destination (mis-scoped in while charting, or exposed by a resolution), **close it** (a closed ticket is unambiguously off the frontier) and leave one line in the **Out of scope** section: the gist plus why it\'s out of scope, linking the closed ticket. It stays out of **Decisions so far**, which records the route actually walked; a scope boundary isn\'t a step on it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Invocation\\r\\n\\r\\nTwo modes. Either way, **never resolve more than one ticket per session**, with the exception of research tickets.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Chart the map\\r\\n\\r\\nUser invokes with a loose idea.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Name the destination.** Call the Skill tool twice, for \\"grilling\\" and \\"domain-modeling\\", to pin down what this map is finding its way to: the spec, decision, or change. The destination fixes the scope, so it\'s settled first.\\r\\n2. **Map the frontier.** Grill again, **breadth-first** this time: fan out across the whole space rather than deep on any one thread, surfacing the open decisions and the first steps takeable now. **If this surfaces no fog** (the way to the destination is already clear, the whole journey small enough for one session), you don\'t need a map. Stop and ask the user how they\'d like to proceed.\\r\\n3. **Create the map** (label `wayfinder:map`): Destination and Notes filled in, Decisions-so-far empty, the fog sketched into **Not yet specified**.\\r\\n4. **Create the tickets you can specify now** as child issues of the map, then wire blocking edges in a **second pass** (issues need ids before they can reference each other). Write cross-references in that pass too, with real ids: a placeholder `#<n>` auto-links to an unrelated issue. Wiring sorts them into the frontier and the blocked; everything you can\'t yet specify stays in the fog: the **Not yet specified** section.\\r\\n5. **Fire the research subagents.** For each `research` ticket you just created, spin up a subagent that calls the Skill tool with \\"research\\" to resolve it in parallel, capturing its findings on a throwaway `research/<name>` branch with a context pointer from the ticket. Push the branch but open no PR: it is never merged.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"6cdecf","wall_time_seconds":0.4181729,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":10485,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 10485)\\nTotal output lines: 562\\n\\n---\\r\\nname: writing-beats\\r\\ndescription: Writing, exploit; assemble raw material into a journey of beats, grounding each term before a beat leans on it.\\r\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n<what-to-do>\\r\\n\\r\\nThe user has passed (or will pass) a markdown file of raw material. This is **exploit**: the exploring is done, the pile is fixed. Commit to a path through it and mine the pile to fill each beat.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user did not say where to save the article, ask once and remember the path.\\r\\n\\r\\nThen run a beat-by-beat journey, choose-your-own-adventure style:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Establish the prerequisites.** Before any beats, settle with the user what the audience already knows walking in: the concepts that are **grounded** from the start. Everything else must be grounded by a beat before a later beat can use it. See [Grounding](#grounding).\\r\\n2. Write 2–3 candidate **starting beats**, drawn from the raw material. Each is a different entry point into the article. Each may only lean on grounded concepts; note what new concepts each one grounds. Show the user the beats before writing to the article file. The user picks one. Preview what beats that pick unlocks, as if the user is seeing a little way down the path.\\r\\n3. Once the user picks a starting beat, write **only that beat** to the article file. A beat may be one sentence or several paragraphs, whatever that beat naturally is. Stop there.\\r\\n4. Re-read the article file from disk. Then offer 2–3 candidate **next beats**: different directions the journey could pivot to from where the article now stands. Each must be reachable from the current grounded set; note what each one grounds.\\r\\n5. Loop steps 3–5 until the article reaches a natural end.\\r\\n\\r\\n</what-to-do>\\r\\n\\r\\n<supporting-info>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Grounding\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery **concept** has to be **grounded** before a beat can lean on it: the audience either walked in knowing it or met it in an earlier beat. A beat that reaches for an ungrounded concept loses the reader; that is the one move the journey can\'t make. The unit is the concept, not the word for it: a beat can lean on an idea the reader lacks even with no jargon in sight. Where a concept has a name (a **term**), grounding it means landing the idea and the term together.\\r\\n\\r\\nA concept gets grounded one of two ways:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Prerequisite**: grounded before the first beat. The audience brings it. Fixed at the start.\\r\\n- **Introduced**: a beat establishes it, and from then on it\'s grounded for every later beat.\\r\\n\\r\\nSo each beat does two jobs: it **requires** concepts that are already grounded, and it **grounds** new ones. Keep a running list of what\'s grounded so far, and update it each time a beat lands.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is what shapes the choose-your-own-adventure. A candidate beat is only reachable if everything it requires is already grounded; picking a beat that grounds concept X unlocks every beat that was waiting on X. When you offer next beats, they must all be reachable from the current grounded set, and say what each one grounds, so the user can see which paths it opens.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe big lever is what you make a prerequisite versus what you ground inside the piece. Demand too much up front and you shut out readers who don\'t have it; ground too much inside and the early beats drown in definitions. Settle this with the user when you establish prerequisites, and revisit it whenever a tempting beat turns out to require a concept nothing has grounded yet: the fix is either a grounding beat before it, or promoting the concept to a prerequisite.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What is a beat\\r\\n\\r\\nA beat is one move in the journey. It does one thing: sets a scene, lands a point, asks a question, drops an aside, twists the angle. Then it stops, leaving the reader at a place where the next beat can pivot.\\r\\n\\r\\nA beat is sized by what it needs:\\r\\n\\r\\n- A single sentence if that\'s all the move is (\\"And then nothing happened for three weeks.\\").\\r\\n- A short paragraph if the move needs setup.\\r\\n- Multiple paragraphs if the beat is a self-contained vignette, argument, or example.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf a \\"beat\\" needs five paragraphs and three subheadings, it\'s not a beat; it\'s two beats glued together. Split it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pulling from the pile\\r\\n\\r\\nPull material from the raw pile to populate each beat. You can paraphrase, split, recombine, or quote. The pile is a quarry.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Ending the journey\\r\\n\\r\\nThe article ends when the journey is complete, not when the pile is empty. Most piles will have leftover fragments that don\'t make it in. That is fine; that is the point of having more raw material than you need.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Writing rhythm\\r\\n\\r\\n- Append one beat at a time. Never write ahead.\\r\\n- Re-read the article file from disk before every write. Preserve user edits absolutely.\\r\\n- If the user edits a previous beat substantially, let it change what comes next.\\r\\n- If the user says \\"rewrite that beat\\" or \\"go back and try a different beat 3\\", do it: edit in place, leave the rest alone.\\r\\n\\r\\n</supporting-info>\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: writing-for-agents\\r\\ndescription: Writing documents for agents. Use when creating or editing skills, or modifying AGENTS.md or CLAUDE.md.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nReference for writing any document an agent consumes: a skill, an `AGENTS.md` / `CLAUDE.md`, a doc reached by a pointer. The packaging differs; the writing does not: the same levers make each one predictable, since the agent takes the same _process_ every run rather than producing the same output.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen the document you\'re writing is a skill, read [`SKILL-MECHANICS.md`](SKILL-MECHANICS.md) for frontmatter, invocation choice, and router skills.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Context pointers\\r\\n\\r\\nA **context pointer** is a reference held in the agent\'s context that names some out-of-context material and encodes the condition for reaching it. A skill\'s description is one; a line in `AGENTS.md` naming a doc is the same object. The pointer\'s _wording_, not its target, decides when the agent reaches the material, and how reliably. A must-have target behind a weakly worded pointer is a variance bug: sharpen the wording first, and inline the material only if sharpening fails.\\r\\n\\r\\nA pointer does two jobs: state what the material is, and list the **branches** that should trigger reaching it (a branch is a distinct case the document handles, so different runs take different paths through it). Every word of an always-loaded pointer costs on every turn, so it earns even harder pruning than the body:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Front-load the leading word**: the pointer is where it does its triggering work.\\r\\n- **One trigger per branch.** Synonyms that rename a single branch are one branch written twice; collapse them and keep only genuinely distinct branches.\\r\\n- **Cut identity the body already carries.**\\r\\n\\r\\n## The two loads\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery document and pointer you add spends one of two budgets:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Context load** is the cost of always-loaded material on the agent\'s window: an `AGENTS.md` line, a skill description, anything sitting in context every turn, spending tokens and attention whether or not it fires.\\r\\n- **Cognitive load** is the cost on the human: which documents exist and when to reach for each. The human is the index. Not a cost to minimise: it is the price of human agency; spend it where human judgement matters, remove it where it does not.\\r\\n\\r\\nMaterial reached only through a pointer escapes context load at the price of the pointer\'s own line; material with no pointer at all rides entirely on cognitive load.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Information hierarchy\\r\\n\\r\\nA document is built from two content types: **steps** (the ordered actions the agent performs) and **reference** (definitions, rules, facts consulted on demand). The two mix freely: all steps (a recipe), all reference (a review\'s rules, this skill), or both. The core decision is where each piece sits on the **information hierarchy**, a ladder ranked by how immediately the agent needs the material:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **In-file step** is the primary tier: what the agent does, in order.\\r\\n2. **In-file reference** is consulted on demand. Often a legitimately flat peer-set (every rule of a review on one rung), which is a fine arrangement, not a smell.\\r\\n3. **Disclosed reference** is pushed out into a separate file, reached by a context pointer, loaded only when the pointer fires. Spans a sibling file in the same folder through fully external reference that lives anywhere and any document can point at.\\r\\n\\r\\nPush too little down and the top bloats; push too much and you hide material the agent actually needs. That tension is the whole decision.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Progressive disclosure** is the move down the ladder (out of the main file and behind a pointer) so the top stays legible. Not primarily a token optimisation: it is how the hierarchy is protected. Branching is the cleanest disclosure test: inline what every branch needs, and push behind a pointer what only some branches reach. When a document has steps, in-file reference that should be disclosed buries them and turns attending to them into a coin-flip: a variance lever, not just a legibility one.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Co-location** is the within-file companion: where the ladder decides _how far down_ a piece sits, co-location decides _what sits beside it_ once there. Keep a concept\'s definition, rules, and caveats under one heading rather than scattered, so reading one part brings its neighbours with it. The test: the document should read like documentation written for the agent. Grouped material reads that way; scattered material does not. (Distinct from duplication: that repeats one meaning in two places; scattering fragments one meaning across many.)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Sprawl** is the failure mode here: a document simply too long, even when every line is live and unique. Attention thins across the excess, and every extra line is one more to keep relevant. The cure is the ladder: disclose reference behind pointers, and split by branch or sequence so each path carries only what it needs.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Steps and completion criteria\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery step ends on a **completion criterion**, the condition that tells the agent the work is done. Two properties make it a lever:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Clarity**: can the agent tell done from not-done? A vague bound (\\"understanding reached\\") invites **premature completion**: ending the step before it is genuinely done, attention slipping to _being done_. The visible steps still ahead (the **post-completion steps**) supply the pull; the criterion\'s clarity is the resistance. Defend in order: **sharpen the bound first** (local and cheap); only if it is irreducibly fuzzy _and_ you observe the rush, hide the later steps by splitting the sequence. Hiding only works across a real context boundary (a hand-off or a subagent dispatch; an inline call leaves the later steps in context and clears nothing).\\r\\n- **Demand**: how much it requires. \\"Every modified model accounted for\\" forces thorough work where \\"produce a change list\\" does not. Demand drives **legwork** (the digging the agent does within the work, latent in the wording rather than written as its own step), and it is not step-bound: \\"every rule applied\\" binds a body of flat reference just as \\"every step done\\" binds a sequence, which is how an all-reference document still carries an exhaustiveness bar.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe strongest criteria are both checkable and exhaustive.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When to split\\r\\n\\r\\nSplitting one document into two spends one of the two loads, so split only when the cut earns it:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **By sequence**: split a run of steps where the post-completion steps tempt the agent to rush the one in front of it. Keeping them out of view drives more legwork on the current task. Beware the reverse: merging sequences exposes each step\'s later steps to what follows, inviting premature completion.\\r\\n- **By invocation**, skill-specific: see [`SKILL-MECHANICS.md`](SKILL-MECHANICS.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Leading words\\r\\n\\r\\nA **leading word** is a compact concept already living in the model\'s pretraining that the agent thinks with while running the document (_lesson_, _fog of war_, _tracer bullets_). Repeated as a token, never as a sentence, it accumulates a distributed definition and anchors a whole region of behaviour in the fewest tokens, by recruiting priors the model already holds. Coining your own works if you define it clearly, but a made-up word recruits no priors: you pay in definition tokens what a pretrained word gives free; reach for an existing word first.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt anchors twice. In the body, _execution_: the agent reaches for the same behaviour every time the word appears, and inside flat reference it focuses attention on a class of thing to look for. In a pointer, _invocation_: when the same word lives in your prompts, your docs, and your codebase, the agent links that shared language to the material and reaches it more reliably.\\r\\n\\r\\nHunt for opportunities to refactor with leading words. A triad spelled out at three sites, a pointer spending a sentence to gesture at one idea. Each is a passage begging to collapse into a single token:\\r\\n\\r\\n- \\"fast, deterministic, low-overhead\\" → _tight_ (a _tight_ loop).\\r\\n- \\"a loop you believe in\\" → _red_, turning a fuzzy gate into a binary observable state (the loop goes _red_ on the bug, or it doesn\'t).\\r\\n\\r\\nYou win twice: fewer tokens, and a sharper hook for the agent to hang its thinking on. Assume every document is carrying restatements that leading words retire. Go find them.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Negation** is the failure mode beside this lever: steering by prohibition drags the forbidden behaviour into context and makes it _more_ available, not less. _Don\'t think of an elephant_, and the elephant is all there is; the negation is a weak modifier the strongly-activated concept overruns, so the ban half-reads as an instruction to do the thing. Prompt the **positive**: state the target behaviour (\\"write one-line comments\\") so the banned one is never spoken. A prohibition earns its place only as a hard guardrail you cannot phrase positively; even then, pair it with the positive target so attention lands on what to do.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pruning\\r\\n\\r\\n- Keep each meaning in a **single source of truth**: one authoritative place, so changing the behaviour is a one-place edit. **Duplication** (the same meaning in more than one place) costs maintenance and tokens, and inflates a meaning\'s prominence on the ladder past its real rank. (The accidental inverse of a leading word, which repeats a token on purpose, never the meaning.)\\r\\n- The **environment** is a source of truth too (`package.json` scripts, config files, the directory layout, `--help` output), and a document that restates it is a **cache**: a copy of a lookup, earning its load only when the lookup is expensive. Cache what the agent cannot find by looking: the unwritten convention, the reason behind a choice, the gotcha no config confesses. Leave the one-file, one-command lookups to the environment, where they cannot go stale.\\r\\n- Check every line for **relevance**: does it still bear on what the document does? A line loses relevance by never bearing on the task (mere exposition, or a branch that should be disclosed) or by going stale as the behaviour or world it describes …2985 tokens truncated…ght_ — one quality restated across a phase — into a single pretrained word (a _tight_ loop).\\r\\n- \\"a loop you believe in\\" -> _red_ — converts a fuzzy gate into a binary observable state (the loop goes _red_ on the bug, or it doesn\'t).\\r\\n\\r\\nYou win twice over: fewer tokens, _and_ a sharper hook for the agent to hang its thinking on. Assume every skill is carrying restatements that leading words retire — go find them.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Failure modes\\r\\n\\r\\nUse these to diagnose issues the user may be having with the skill.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Premature completion** — ending a step before it\'s genuinely done, attention slipping to _being done_. Defence, in order: sharpen the completion criterion first (cheap, local); only if it is irreducibly fuzzy _and_ you observe the rush, hide the post-completion steps by splitting (the sequence cut).\\r\\n- **Duplication** — the same meaning in more than one place. Costs maintenance and tokens, and inflates a meaning\'s prominence on the ladder past its real rank.\\r\\n- **Sediment** — stale layers that settle because adding feels safe and removing feels risky. The default fate of any skill without a pruning discipline.\\r\\n- **Sprawl** — a skill simply too long, even when every line is live and unique. Hurts readability and maintainability and wastes tokens. The cure is the ladder: disclose **reference** behind pointers, and split by **branch** or sequence so each path carries only what it needs.\\r\\n- **No-op** — a line the model already obeys by default, so you pay load to say nothing. The test: does it change behaviour versus the default? A weak leading word (_be thorough_ when the agent is already thorough-ish) is a no-op; the fix is a stronger word (_relentless_), not a different technique.\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: writing-plans\\r\\ndescription: Use when you have a spec or requirements for a multi-step task, before touching code\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Writing Plans\\r\\n\\r\\n## Overview\\r\\n\\r\\nWrite comprehensive implementation plans assuming the engineer has zero context for our codebase and questionable taste. Document everything they need to know: which files to touch for each task, code, testing, docs they might need to check, how to test it. Give them the whole plan as bite-sized tasks. DRY. YAGNI. TDD. Frequent commits.\\r\\n\\r\\nAssume they are a skilled developer, but know almost nothing about our toolset or problem domain. Assume they don\'t know good test design very well.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Announce at start:** \\"I\'m using the writing-plans skill to create the implementation plan.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n**Context:** If working in an isolated worktree, it should have been created via the `superpowers:using-git-worktrees` skill at execution time.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Save plans to:** `docs/superpowers/plans/YYYY-MM-DD-<feature-name>.md`\\r\\n- (User preferences for plan location override this default)\\r\\n\\r\\n## Scope Check\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the spec covers multiple independent subsystems, it should have been broken into sub-project specs during brainstorming. If it wasn\'t, suggest breaking this into separate plans — one per subsystem. Each plan should produce working, testable software on its own.\\r\\n\\r\\n## File Structure\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore defining tasks, map out which files will be created or modified and what each one is responsible for. This is where decomposition decisions get locked in.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Design units with clear boundaries and well-defined interfaces. Each file should have one clear responsibility.\\r\\n- You reason best about code you can hold in context at once, and your edits are more reliable when files are focused. Prefer smaller, focused files over large ones that do too much.\\r\\n- Files that change together should live together. Split by responsibility, not by technical layer.\\r\\n- In existing codebases, follow established patterns. If the codebase uses large files, don\'t unilaterally restructure - but if a file you\'re modifying has grown unwieldy, including a split in the plan is reasonable.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis structure informs the task decomposition. Each task should produce self-contained changes that make sense independently.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Task Right-Sizing\\r\\n\\r\\nA task is the smallest unit that carries its own test cycle and is worth a\\r\\nfresh reviewer\'s gate. When drawing task boundaries: fold setup,\\r\\nconfiguration, scaffolding, and documentation steps into the task whose\\r\\ndeliverable needs them; split only where a reviewer could meaningfully\\r\\nreject one task while approving its neighbor. Each task ends with an\\r\\nindependently testable deliverable.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Bite-Sized Task Granularity\\r\\n\\r\\n**Each step is one action (2-5 minutes):**\\r\\n- \\"Write the failing test\\" - step\\r\\n- \\"Run it to make sure it fails\\" - step\\r\\n- \\"Implement the minimal code to make the test pass\\" - step\\r\\n- \\"Run the tests and make sure they pass\\" - step\\r\\n- \\"Commit\\" - step\\r\\n\\r\\n## Plan Document Header\\r\\n\\r\\n**Every plan MUST start with this header:**\\r\\n\\r\\n```markdown\\r\\n# [Feature Name] Implementation Plan\\r\\n\\r\\n> **For agentic workers:** REQUIRED SUB-SKILL: Use superpowers:subagent-driven-development (recommended) or superpowers:executing-plans to implement this plan task-by-task. Steps use checkbox (`- [ ]`) syntax for tracking.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Goal:** [One sentence describing what this builds]\\r\\n\\r\\n**Architecture:** [2-3 sentences about approach]\\r\\n\\r\\n**Tech Stack:** [Key technologies/libraries]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Global Constraints\\r\\n\\r\\n[The spec\'s project-wide requirements — version floors, dependency limits,\\r\\nnaming and copy rules, platform requirements — one line each, with exact\\r\\nvalues copied verbatim from the spec. Every task\'s requirements implicitly\\r\\ninclude this section.]\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Task Structure\\r\\n\\r\\n````markdown\\r\\n### Task N: [Component Name]\\r\\n\\r\\n**Files:**\\r\\n- Create: `exact/path/to/file.py`\\r\\n- Modify: `exact/path/to/existing.py:123-145`\\r\\n- Test: `tests/exact/path/to/test.py`\\r\\n\\r\\n**Interfaces:**\\r\\n- Consumes: [what this task uses from earlier tasks — exact signatures]\\r\\n- Produces: [what later tasks rely on — exact function names, parameter\\r\\n  and return types. A task\'s implementer sees only their own task; this\\r\\n  block is how they learn the names and types neighboring tasks use.]\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing test**\\r\\n\\r\\n```python\\r\\ndef test_specific_behavior():\\r\\n    result = function(input)\\r\\n    assert result == expected\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] **Step 2: Run test to verify it fails**\\r\\n\\r\\nRun: `pytest tests/path/test.py::test_name -v`\\r\\nExpected: FAIL with \\"function not defined\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] **Step 3: Write minimal implementation**\\r\\n\\r\\n```python\\r\\ndef function(input):\\r\\n    return expected\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] **Step 4: Run test to verify it passes**\\r\\n\\r\\nRun: `pytest tests/path/test.py::test_name -v`\\r\\nExpected: PASS\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] **Step 5: Commit**\\r\\n\\r\\n```bash\\r\\ngit add tests/path/test.py src/path/file.py\\r\\ngit commit -m \\"feat: add specific feature\\"\\r\\n```\\r\\n````\\r\\n\\r\\n## No Placeholders\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery step must contain the actual content an engineer needs. These are **plan failures** — never write them:\\r\\n- \\"TBD\\", \\"TODO\\", \\"implement later\\", \\"fill in details\\"\\r\\n- \\"Add appropriate error handling\\" / \\"add validation\\" / \\"handle edge cases\\"\\r\\n- \\"Write tests for the above\\" (without actual test code)\\r\\n- \\"Similar to Task N\\" (repeat the code — the engineer may be reading tasks out of order)\\r\\n- Steps that describe what to do without showing how (code blocks required for code steps)\\r\\n- References to types, functions, or methods not defined in any task\\r\\n\\r\\n## Remember\\r\\n- Exact file paths always\\r\\n- Complete code in every step — if a step changes code, show the code\\r\\n- Exact commands with expected output\\r\\n- DRY, YAGNI, TDD, frequent commits\\r\\n\\r\\n## Self-Review\\r\\n\\r\\nAfter writing the complete plan, look at the spec with fresh eyes and check the plan against it. This is a checklist you run yourself — not a subagent dispatch.\\r\\n\\r\\n**1. Spec coverage:** Skim each section/requirement in the spec. Can you point to a task that implements it? List any gaps.\\r\\n\\r\\n**2. Placeholder scan:** Search your plan for red flags — any of the patterns from the \\"No Placeholders\\" section above. Fix them.\\r\\n\\r\\n**3. Type consistency:** Do the types, method signatures, and property names you used in later tasks match what you defined in earlier tasks? A function called `clearLayers()` in Task 3 but `clearFullLayers()` in Task 7 is a bug.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you find issues, fix them inline. No need to re-review — just fix and move on. If you find a spec requirement with no task, add the task.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Execution Handoff\\r\\n\\r\\nAfter saving the plan, offer execution choice:\\r\\n\\r\\n**\\"Plan complete and saved to `docs/superpowers/plans/<filename>.md`. Two execution options:**\\r\\n\\r\\n**1. Subagent-Driven (recommended)** - I dispatch a fresh subagent per task, review between tasks, fast iteration\\r\\n\\r\\n**2. Inline Execution** - Execute tasks in this session using executing-plans, batch execution with checkpoints\\r\\n\\r\\n**Which approach?\\"**\\r\\n\\r\\n**If Subagent-Driven chosen:**\\r\\n- **REQUIRED SUB-SKILL:** Use superpowers:subagent-driven-development\\r\\n- Fresh subagent per task + two-stage review\\r\\n\\r\\n**If Inline Execution chosen:**\\r\\n- **REQUIRED SUB-SKILL:** Use superpowers:executing-plans\\r\\n- Batch execution with checkpoints for review\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: writing-shape\\r\\ndescription: \\"Writing, exploit: shape raw material into an article, paragraph by paragraph.\\"\\r\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n<what-to-do>\\r\\n\\r\\nThe user has passed (or will pass) a markdown file of raw material. Treat it as the input pile: anything from a tidy list of fragments to a wall of unstructured prose to a transcript. The format does not matter. Read it end-to-end before doing anything else.\\r\\n\\r\\nThen run a shaping session that produces a separate article document. This is **exploit**: the exploring is done, the pile is fixed: commit to a structure and mine the pile to fill it. Do not edit the raw material file: it is read-only to this skill.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user did not say where to save the article, ask once and remember the path.\\r\\n\\r\\n</what-to-do>\\r\\n\\r\\n<supporting-info>\\r\\n\\r\\n## The loop\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Read the pile.** Read the input file in full. Form a sense of what\'s in it.\\r\\n2. **Establish the prerequisites.** Settle with the user what the reader knows walking in: the concepts that are **grounded** from the start. Everything else must be grounded by a block before a later block can lean on it. See [Grounding](#grounding).\\r\\n3. **Draft 2–3 candidate openings.** Each opening should imply a different thesis or angle for the article. Show all of them. Force the user to pick or compose a hybrid. The chosen opening defines what the rest of the article must do.\\r\\n4. **Grow paragraph by paragraph.** After the opening lands, ask \\"given this opening, what does the reader need to hear next?\\" Pull material from the pile to answer. The next block may only lean on grounded concepts, and grounds new ones as it lands. Argue about the form the next block takes: a paragraph, a list, a table, a callout, a quote, a code block. Each format choice should be deliberate and defensible.\\r\\n5. **Append to the article file as you go.** Don\'t batch. Write each agreed paragraph or block immediately so the user can see the article taking shape.\\r\\n6. **Loop step 4 until the article is done.** The user decides when it\'s done.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Grounding\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery **concept** has to be **grounded** before a block can lean on it: the reader either walked in knowing it or met it in an earlier block. A block that reaches for an ungrounded concept loses the reader. The unit is the concept, not the word for it: a block can lean on an idea the reader lacks even with no jargon in sight. Where a concept has a name (a **term**), grounding it means landing the idea and the term together.\\r\\n\\r\\nA concept gets grounded one of two ways:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Prerequisite**: grounded before the opening. The reader brings it. Fixed at the start.\\r\\n- **Introduced**: a block establishes it, and from then on it\'s grounded for the rest of the article.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep a running list of what\'s grounded. When you ask \\"what does the reader need to hear next?\\", an ungrounded concept the next move needs is itself the answer: ground it first (here or in an earlier block) or you can\'t make the move. This is the gap-naming of [Pulling from the pile](#pulling-from-the-pile) one level up: there the pile is missing material; here the article is missing a foundation.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe lever is what you make a prerequisite versus what you ground inside the article. Demand too much up front and you shut readers out; ground too much inside and the opening drowns in definitions. Settle it with the user when you establish prerequisites.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Conversational feel\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is a grilling session inverted. In ideation, the question was \\"what are you actually noticing?\\" Here it\'s \\"what is this article actually arguing, and in what order does the reader need to hear it?\\" Push back. Refuse to let weak transitions slide. If a paragraph doesn\'t earn its place, cut it.\\r\\n\\r\\nSpecific moves to keep using:\\r\\n\\r\\n- \\"What does this paragraph do for the reader that the previous one didn\'t?\\"\\r\\n- \\"If I cut this, what breaks?\\"\\r\\n- \\"Is this prose, or should it be a list? Why prose?\\"\\r\\n- \\"This sentence is doing two jobs: split it or pick one.\\"\\r\\n- \\"The opening promised X. We\'ve drifted to Y. Either re-thread it or change the opening.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pulling from the pile\\r\\n\\r\\nTreat the raw material as a quarry, not a script. Pull a fragment, rework it to fit the surrounding paragraph, and place it. A fragment may be split across multiple paragraphs, merged with another, or paraphrased. The pile\'s job is to be mined; the article\'s job is to read as one voice.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the pile lacks something the article needs, name the gap explicitly: \\"We need an example here and the pile doesn\'t have one. Give me one now or we cut this section.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n## Format arguments to actually have\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen choosing how to render a block, weigh these tradeoffs out loud with the user, not silently:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Prose vs. list.** Prose carries argument; lists carry parallel items. If items aren\'t truly parallel, prose is better. If they are, a list is faster to scan.\\r\\n- **Inline vs. callout.** Tips, warnings, and asides go in callouts (`> [!TIP]`, `> [!NOTE]`), but only if they\'d genuinely derail the main argument inline. Otherwise leave them inline.\\r\\n- **Table vs. repeated structure.** If the same shape repeats 3+ times with the same fields, a table. Otherwise prose with bold leads.\\r\\n- **Quote vs. paraphrase.** Quote when the original wording is the point. Paraphrase when only the idea matters.\\r\\n- **Code block vs. inline code.** Multi-line, runnable, or illustrative → block. Single token or identifier → inline.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Writing rhythm\\r\\n\\r\\nAppend to the article file as each block is agreed. Re-read the file from disk before every write: the user may have edited between turns. Never overwrite blindly. If the user wants a paragraph rewritten, edit that specific paragraph in place; leave the rest alone.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Out of scope\\r\\n\\r\\n- Mining for new fragments that aren\'t in the pile (handle gaps as in \\"Pulling from the pile\\").\\r\\n- Editing the raw material file.\\r\\n- Publishing, formatting for a specific platform, or adding frontmatter the user didn\'t ask for.\\r\\n\\r\\n</supporting-info>\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

(The accidental inverse of a leading word, which repeats a token on purpose, never the meaning.)\\r\\n- The **environment** is a source of truth too (`package.json` scripts, config files, the directory layout, `--help` output), and a document that restates it is a **cache**: a copy of a lookup, earning its load only when the lookup is expensive. When a sentence fails, delete the whole sentence rather than trim words from it. The test also grades leading words: a word too weak to beat the default (_be thorough_ when the agent is already thorough-ish) is a no-op, and the fix is a stronger word (_relentless_), not a different technique.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"a63934","wall_time_seconds":0.4215185,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3196,"output":"---\\r\\nname: writing-fragments\\r\\ndescription: \\"Writing, explore: mine raw fragments, no structure yet.\\"\\r\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n<what-to-do>\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is pure **explore**: widen the space of what could be written without committing to structure. Committing is _exploit_, a separate skill\'s job. Run a grilling session that produces fragments, interviewing the user relentlessly about whatever they want to write about. Imposing phases, outlines, or article structure is out of scope here.\\r\\n\\r\\nAs fragments emerge from either side of the conversation, append them to a single markdown file.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user did not pass a path, ask once where to save the document, then remember it for the rest of the session.\\r\\n\\r\\nCapture fragments from the very first thing the user says, including the initial prompt.\\r\\n\\r\\nOn first write, put a single H1 at the top with a working title (it can change later) and nothing else: no metadata, no TOC, no date.\\r\\n\\r\\n</what-to-do>\\r\\n\\r\\n<supporting-info>\\r\\n\\r\\n## What is a fragment\\r\\n\\r\\nA fragment is any piece of text that might survive into the final article. It must be _readable by the author_ (the author can tell what it means), but it does not need to define its terms or be comprehensible to a cold reader. The bar is \\"is this a piece of good writing?\\", not \\"is this a self-contained argument?\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nFragments are deliberately heterogeneous. Examples of what could be a fragment:\\r\\n\\r\\n- A sharp sentence you\'d want to deploy somewhere but don\'t yet know where.\\r\\n- A claim with a one-line justification.\\r\\n- A vignette: a thing that happened, a code snippet, a scenario, an analogy.\\r\\n- A half-thought: \\"something about how X feels like Y, work this out later.\\"\\r\\n- A quote, a piece of dialogue, an overheard line.\\r\\n- A list of related observations that hang together by feel.\\r\\n- A complaint, a confession, a punchline.\\r\\n- A **leading word**: a compact metaphor or coinage the whole piece can hang on (one term that names the idea, the way _tracer bullets_ or _fog of war_ names a whole pattern).\\r\\n\\r\\nOf these, the leading word is the most valuable fragment to land. It is load-bearing: name the right one in explore and it shapes the structure, the transitions, and the title later, paying dividends through the entire exploit phase. When the conversation circles a recurring idea, push to coin a word for it.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe novelist\'s diary is the model: years of unstructured noticings that later get mined for raw material. Fragments are noticings.\\r\\n\\r\\n## File format\\r\\n\\r\\n```markdown\\r\\n# Working title\\r\\n\\r\\nA first fragment lives here.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt can be multiple paragraphs. It can include lists, code, quotes: whatever\\r\\nshape the fragment naturally takes.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nA second fragment.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n> A quoted line that the user wants to keep around.\\r\\n\\r\\nA reaction to it.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n- A cluster of related observations\\r\\n- That hang together by feel\\r\\n- And want to be near each other\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nFragments are separated by a horizontal rule (`\\\\n---\\\\n`). No headings inside the body. No tags. No order beyond the order they were added.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Writing rhythm\\r\\n\\r\\nAppend silently. Don\'t ask permission for each fragment. Mention what you added in passing (\\"adding that\\"), but don\'t interrupt the conversation with save dialogs.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore every write: re-read the file from disk. The user may have edited, reordered, or deleted fragments between turns, so preserve their changes. Never overwrite the file; only append (or, if the user asks, edit a specific fragment in place).\\r\\n\\r\\nThe user can say \\"cut the last one\\", \\"rewrite that one sharper\\", \\"merge those two\\" at any time. Treat those as first-class instructions.\\r\\n\\r\\n</supporting-info>\\r\\n---\\r\\nname: writing-great-skills\\r\\ndescription: Reference for writing and editing skills well — the vocabulary and principles that make a skill predictable.\\r\\ndisable-model-invocation: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nA skill exists to wrangle determinism out of a stochastic system. **Predictability** — the agent taking the same _process_ every run, not producing the same output — is the root virtue; every lever below serves it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Bold terms** are defined in [`GLOSSARY.md`](GLOSSARY.md); look them up there for the full meaning.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Invocation\\r\\n\\r\\nTwo choices, trading different costs:\\r\\n\\r\\n- A **model-invoked** skill keeps a **description**, so the agent can fire it autonomously _and_ other skills can reach it (you can still type its name too). It contributes to **context load** — the description sits in the window every turn. Mechanics: omit `disable-model-invocation`, and write a model-facing description with rich trigger phrasing (\\"Use when the user wants…, mentions…\\").\\r\\n- A **user-invoked** skill strips the description from the agent\'s reach: only you, typing its name, can invoke it — and no other skill can. Zero context load, but it spends **cognitive load**: _you_ are the index that must remember it exists. Mechanics: set `disable-model-invocation: true`; the `description` becomes human-facing — a one-line summary, trigger lists stripped.\\r\\n\\r\\nPick model-invocation only when the agent must reach the skill on its own, or another skill must. If it only ever fires by hand, make it user-invoked and pay no context load.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen user-invoked skills multiply past what you can remember, that piled-up cognitive load is cured by a **router skill**: one user-invoked skill that names the others and when to reach for each.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Writing the description\\r\\n\\r\\nA model-invoked **description** does two jobs — state what the skill is, and list the **branches** that should trigger it. Every word increases **context load**, so a description earns even harder pruning than the body:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Front-load the skill\'s leading word** — the description is where it does its invocation work.\\r\\n- **One trigger per branch.** Synonyms that rename a single branch are **duplication** — \\"build features using TDD … asks for test-first development\\" is one branch written twice. Collapse them; keep only genuinely distinct branches.\\r\\n- **Cut identity that\'s already in the body.** Keep the description to triggers, plus any \\"when another skill needs…\\" reach clause.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Information hierarchy\\r\\n\\r\\nA skill is built from two content types — **steps** and **reference** — that mix freely: a skill can be all steps, all reference, or both. The core decision is which to use and where each sits on the **information hierarchy**, a ladder ranked by how immediately the agent needs the material:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **In-skill step** — an ordered action in `SKILL.md`, the primary tier: what the agent does, in order. Each step ends on a **completion criterion**, the condition that tells the agent the work is done. Make it _checkable_ (can the agent tell done from not-done?) and, where it matters, _exhaustive_ (\\"every modified model accounted for\\", not \\"produce a change list\\") — a vague criterion invites **premature completion**.\\r\\n2. **In-skill reference** — a definition, rule, or fact in `SKILL.md`, consulted on demand. Often a legitimately flat peer-set (every rule of a review on one rung) — a fine arrangement, not a smell. _This skill is all reference._\\r\\n3. **External reference** — reference pushed out of `SKILL.md` into a separate file, reached by a **context pointer**, loaded only when the pointer fires. (Spans _disclosed_ reference — a sibling file like `GLOSSARY.md`, still part of the skill — through fully **external reference** that lives outside the skill system and any skill can point at.)\\r\\n\\r\\nA demanding completion criterion drives thorough **legwork** — the digging the agent does within the work — whether the skill has steps or not, since \\"every rule applied\\" binds flat reference just as \\"every step done\\" binds a sequence.\\r\\n\\r\\nPush too little down and the top bloats; push too much and you hide material the agent actually needs. That tension is the whole decision.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Progressive disclosure** is the move down the ladder — out of `SKILL.md` into a linked file — so the top stays legible. Mechanics: a linked `.md` file in the skill folder, named for what it holds (this skill discloses its full definitions to `GLOSSARY.md`). Some skills are used in more than one way, and each distinct way is a **branch** — different runs taking different paths through the skill. Branching is the cleanest disclosure test: inline what every branch needs, and push behind a pointer what only some branches reach. A **context pointer**\'s _wording_, not its target, decides when and how reliably the agent reaches the material.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhere the ladder decides _how far down_ a piece sits, **co-location** decides _what sits beside it_ once there: keep a concept\'s definition, rules, and caveats under one heading rather than scattered, so reading one part brings its neighbours with it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When to split\\r\\n\\r\\n**Granularity** is how finely you divide skills, and each cut spends one of the two loads, so split only when the cut earns it. Two cuts:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **By invocation** — split off a **model-invoked** skill when you have a distinct **leading word** that should trigger it on its own, or another skill must reach it. You pay **context load** for the new always-loaded **description**, so that independent reach has to be worth it.\\r\\n- **By sequence** — split a run of **steps** when the steps still ahead (a step\'s **post-completion steps**) tempt the agent to rush the one in front of it (**premature completion**). Keeping them out of view encourages the agent to do more **legwork** on the current task.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pruning\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep each meaning in a **single source of truth**: one authoritative place, so changing the behaviour is a one-place edit.\\r\\n\\r\\nCheck every line for **relevance**: does it still bear on what the skill does?\\r\\n\\r\\nThen hunt **no-ops** sentence by sentence, not just line by line: run the no-op test on each sentence in isolation, and when one fails, delete the whole sentence rather than trim words from it. Be aggressive — most prose that fails should go, not be rewritten.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Leading words\\r\\n\\r\\nA **leading word** is a compact concept already living in the model\'s pretraining that the agent thinks with while running the skill (e.g. _lesson_, _fog of war_, _tracer bullets_). Repeated throughout the text (though not necessarily - a strong leading word might only be needed once), it accumulates a distributed definition and anchors a whole region of behaviour in the fewest tokens, by recruiting priors the model already holds.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt serves predictability twice. In the body it anchors _execution_: the agent reaches for the same behaviour every time the word appears. In the description it anchors _invocation_: when the same word lives in your prompts, docs, and code, the agent links that shared language to the skill and fires it more reliably.\\r\\n\\r\\nHunt for opportunities to refactor skills to use leading words. A triad spelled out at three sites (**duplication**), a description spending a sentence to gesture at one idea — each is a passage begging to **collapse** into a single token. Examples include:\\r\\n\\r\\n- \\"fast, deterministic, low-overhead\\" -> _tight_ — one quality restated across a phase — into a single pretrained word (a _tight_ loop).\\r\\n- \\"a loop you believe in\\" -> _red_ — converts a fuzzy gate into a binary observable state (the loop goes _red_ on the bug, or it doesn\'t).\\r\\n\\r\\nYou win twice over: fewer tokens, _and_ a sharper hook for the agent to hang its thinking on. Assume every skill is carrying restatements that leading words retire — go find them.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Failure modes\\r\\n\\r\\nUse these to diagnose issues the user may be having with the skill.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Premature completion** — ending a step before it\'s genuinely done, attention slipping to _being done_. Defence, in order: sharpen the completion criterion first (cheap, local); only if it is irreducibly fuzzy _and_ you observe the rush, hide the post-completion steps by splitting (the sequence cut).\\r\\n- **Duplication** — the same meaning in more than one place. Costs maintenance and tokens, and inflates a meaning\'s prominence on the ladder past its real rank.\\r\\n- **Sediment** — stale layers that settle because adding feels safe and removing feels risky. The default fate of any skill without a pruning discipline.\\r\\n- **Sprawl** — a skill simply too long, even when every line is live and unique. Hurts readability and maintainability and wastes tokens. The cure is the ladder: disclose **reference** behind pointers, and split by **branch** or sequence so each path carries only what it needs.\\r\\n- **No-op** — a line the model already obeys by default, so you pay load to say nothing. The test: does it change behaviour versus the default? A weak leading word (_be thorough_ when the agent is already thorough-ish) is a no-op; the fix is a stronger word (_relentless_), not a different technique.\\r\\n"}'}]

On any major change, mirror it into CLAUDE.md so Claude and Codex stay in sync. -->\\r\\n# Jay 2 — operating instructions for Codex (ai-os)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe canonical root is the repository directory containing this file (`C:\\\\Users\\\\Joshu\\\\dev\\\\ai-os` on this Windows machine). Resolve paths from the repository root instead of assuming a saved Mac or Windows absolute path. This is the router: it holds pointers, not knowledge. Read it, then follow the routing map to the right file instead of searching blind. Twin for Claude Code: `CLAUDE.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Identity and mission\\r\\n\\r\\nYou are **Jay 2**, Joshua\'s executive assistant. This root is his EA workspace and command center. Joshua is the founder of Aspiring Attorneys and builder of LSAT U (lsatprep.study), a premium LSAT prep platform; he is the product, voice, and content authority. Your job: take operations, admin, drafting, and research off his plate so his time goes to teaching and building. Frame work as decisions and tradeoffs, not basics.\\r\\n\\r\\nStanding duties, run from here without re-briefing: student operations (transcripts, assignments, follow-ups via `students/`), prospect and consultation correspondence (drafted in his voice, **never auto-sent unless a pre-approved template**), platform actions on lsatprep.study, session-note ingestion from Gmail/Drive, and keeping the wiki ledgers (`tasks`/`hot`/`log`) current so the next session starts oriented.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Routing map\\r\\n\\r\\nPaths are relative to the repository root.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Need | Path |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| **Who Joshua is** (identity, preferences, contact) | `context/about-me.md` |\\r\\n| **The business** (offers, money, channels, gaps) | `context/work.md` |\\r\\n| **Current priorities** (the 90-day slate) | `context/priorities.md` |\\r\\n| **What we decided and why** (locked decisions) | `decisions/log.md` |\\r\\n| Freshest session state (hot cache) | `wiki/hot.md` |\\r\\n| LLM Wiki schema (source, page, ingest/query/lint rules) | `system/schema/llm-wiki.md` |\\r\\n| Master index · work ledger · dated history | `wiki/index.md` · `wiki/tasks.md` · `wiki/log.md` |\\r\\n| Product ground truth (pointers to live backends) | `wiki/dashboard.md` |\\r\\n| LSAT teaching knowledge | `corpus/index.md` |\\r\\n| A student — profile, transcripts, work, correspondence | `students/<name>/` — start at `students/README.md` |\\r\\n| Voice for anything a student or prospect reads | `wiki/teaching-voice.md` |\\r\\n| Was it sent? (every outbound draft/send) | `wiki/send-ledger.md` — append-only; Gmail is authority |\\r\\n| Who the students are (name, email, sessions, links) | `students/roster.generated.md` — regenerate, never hand-edit |\\r\\n| Learned facts and preferences (automemory) | native store `~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/` + `MEMORY.md` index |\\r\\n| Skill / agent catalog | `skills/INDEX.md`, `agents/` |\\r\\n| Systems Jay 2 can reach (registry) | `connections.md` — 7 data domains, mechanism/auth/freshness; read by `/audit` |\\r\\n| Operator brain (how to think about automation) | `references/3ms-framework.md` — the Three Ms; walked weekly by `/level-up` |\\r\\n| Automation build backlog (what to build next, ranked) | `references/automation-backlog.md` — corpus-derived gaps + tiers; feeds `/level-up` |\\r\\n| How-it-works reference notes (methods, not state) | `references/` — also `references/claude-plus-codex.md`, `references/obsidian-command-center.md`, `references/second-brain-levels.md` |\\r\\n| Web research and Firecrawl agents | `references/firecrawl.md` (guidelines in `references/firecrawl/`) |\\r\\n| Shared Windows CLI tools for Codex, Claude Code, and Antigravity | `references/ai-workspace/windows-cli-toolchain.md` — installed paths, PATH refresh, and usage for `uv`, `yt-dlp`, FFmpeg, and Deno |\\r\\n| Immutable source evidence · intake queue | `raw/sources/` · `raw/inbox/` — one inbox, one archive |\\r\\n| Archived web clips, with provenance + hashes | `raw/sources/clips/<slug>/` — clip + `PROVENANCE.md` + `SHA256SUMS` |\\r\\n| Raw personal-data exports (Google Takeout, Gemini session transcripts) | `raw/data-about-me/` — unprocessed PII, git-ignored; never read wholesale |\\r\\n| Design/plan thinking, dated (grill-me + interview outputs) | `brainstorms/` — supersedes the retired `aios/` reform folder |\\r\\n| Session handoffs · chat exports | `handoffs/` — operational records, deliberately OUTSIDE `raw/`; `session-handoff` writes here |\\r\\n| Current local work in Claude/Codex cloud | `setup/github-sync/README.md`; private `codex/local-sync/<machine>/<checkout>` branches and generated `.cloud-sync/` metadata/conversation exports. Cloud edits use separate task branches. |\\r\\n| External teardowns and research, dated | `research/` |\\r\\n| Web-clipped articles (Obsidian Web Clipper) | `raw/inbox/` — intake queue; `ingest` copies and hash-verifies clips into `raw/sources/clips/`; processed originals stay until removal is authorized, so inbox presence alone does not mean unprocessed |\\r\\n| Application code | `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` — own repo, own instructions, own remote |\\r\\n| Other projects (life-path, …) | `../ai-os-projects/<name>/` — each with its own `CLAUDE.md` + `.claude/` |\\r\\n\\r\\nApplication repositories and dependencies stay outside this Obsidian vault, in sibling `../ai-os-projects/`. Do not create a junction from the vault back into that directory.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Retrieval order\\r\\n\\r\\n1. `wiki/hot.md` — current-session narrative and open threads.\\r\\n2. `wiki/index.md` → the page (teaching knowledge: `corpus/index.md`; a student: their folder). Follow links; there is no semantic search and none is needed at this size.\\r\\n3. The live system — the final freshness escalation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Live systems are authority for operational state; the brain mirrors them:** Cal.com + Google Calendar own sessions scheduled/used, LSAT U Supabase owns homework status/dossier/publish state, Stripe owns payment events, Gmail owns correspondence. On any disagreement, the backend wins. The brain is primary — nothing upstream holds them — for transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, curriculum, product specs, and decisions.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Do not read the wiki unless the task needs it.** Retrieval costs context every turn. Skip it for: code edits in `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/`; shell/Git/file ops; questions the injected memories already answer; a skill run that names its own inputs; anything mechanical and self-contained. Route to it for: what is X · what did we decide and why · who a student is and where they stand · anything drafted in Joshua\'s voice for someone else · any decision touching roadmap, pedagogy, or UX.\\r\\n\\r\\n**LLM Wiki schema:** For knowledge intake, knowledge questions, durable synthesis, and wiki maintenance, follow `system/schema/llm-wiki.md`. Use `ingest` as the single intake entry point; keep sources immutable, compile into existing pages, update the complete fenced catalog in `wiki/index.md`, and append changes to `wiki/log.md`. Preserve domain homes and live-system authority. Mechanical tasks retain the retrieval exceptions above.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Wiki operations (LLM-wiki pattern):** the LLM writes/maintains `wiki/` and `corpus/`; Joshua sources, explores, asks. Raw sources are immutable — read, never modify. *Ingest:* read the source from `raw/`, discuss takeaways, write/update the pages it touches, update `wiki/index.md`, append to `wiki/log.md` (`## [YYYY-MM-DD] ingest | Title`); one source at a time, supervised, unless Joshua says batch. *Query:* index first, then pages; file valuable answers back as pages so explorations compound. *Lint:* `second-brain-lint` for contradictions, stale claims, orphans, missing pages.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Voice\\r\\n\\r\\n- **No em-dashes, ever.** Joshua never types them; a stray em-dash reads as AI and costs trust.\\r\\n- **No AI-slop vocabulary** — never \\"delve,\\" \\"tapestry,\\" \\"testament,\\" \\"demystify,\\" \\"boasts,\\" \\"in the realm of,\\" and their kin.\\r\\n- **Anything a student or prospect will read** routes through `wiki/teaching-voice.md` and is drafted, never sent, unless it is a pre-approved template.\\r\\n- **LSAT teaching:** use project-owned `lsat-u-voice` and `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`; this Joshua-selected standard takes precedence over older vendor voice blends.\\r\\n- **When an outbound message is authorized**, preserve the draft\'s paragraph breaks, list lines, and intentional spacing. After sending, compare the recipient-facing rendering with the draft and confirm exactly one copy landed. Report any mismatch; never resend automatically.\\r\\n- **Lead with your recommendation, not a menu.** Be decisive; don\'t over-hedge.\\r\\n- **Any prompt Joshua will paste elsewhere** goes inside a fenced code block, never inline prose.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Tools, keys, and secrets\\r\\n\\r\\n- **The entire secret model is `.env` + `.gitignore`.** The root `.env` (git-ignored — verify with `git check-ignore .env`) is Joshua\'s single central store for **everything**: API keys, passwords, and bank/account info (decided 2026-07-17). OAuth client secrets live in `~/.config/gws/`, outside the repo. There is no vault, no Keychain, no encrypted store (`records.sparsebundle` is dead) and none is wanted. Never paste a credential into chat — add it to `.env` directly in the editor.\\r\\n- **Safety is the tool layer, not prompts:** allow/deny tool lists + scoped API keys. Jay 2 acts under its own credentials and never impersonates Joshua.\\r\\n- **Connected live systems:** Cal.com, Google Calendar, LSAT U Supabase, Stripe, Gmail. Full registry in `connections.md`.\\r\\n- **Google access is `gws` CLI only** (`gws gmail|drive|calendar|docs …`, keyring-auth; see `skills/codex/gws-shared/`). Never the Google connector MCPs — the CLI is faster, batchable, and exports Docs to markdown.\\r\\n- **Shared Windows command tools:** use `uv`, `yt-dlp`, `ffmpeg`, `ffprobe`, `ffplay`, and `deno` from the Windows user PATH. For versions, locations, how to invoke them, or a stale terminal environment, read `references/ai-workspace/windows-cli-toolchain.md`. Check before reinstalling; restart the affected IDE when its inherited PATH is stale.\\r\\n- **Government IDs stay outside this OS.** Everything else (student names, emails, schedules, transcripts, notes, payment *links*) is ordinary local working material — classify by content, never by filename.\\r\\n- **Git remote is enabled.** `origin` is GitHub (`vnjkoxpl-42905/ai-os`, decided 2026-09-29). The outer repo never tracks `.env`, `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` (own repo), runtime logs, or locks. Do not create gitlinks.\\r\\n- **New laptop restore:** GitHub contains tracked ai-os files and skills. It deliberately excludes `.env`, `raw/`, app repos, credentials, and machine sessions. Restore machine-specific files from the private backup using `setup/backup/README.md` and its `README - START HERE.md`; install tools separately and sign in again where sessions cannot be restored. Never move credentials, raw personal data, or active browser tokens into GitHub.\\r\\n- **Authorized cloud transfer (2026-10-07):** private sync branches also carry portable skills, memory and conversation exports. Joshua explicitly authorized credential transfer; implementation uses the authenticated encrypted package described in `setup/github-sync/README.md`; plaintext secrets and its unlock key stay outside GitHub and Drive. Cloud authentication and native browser sessions still require separate verification.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Skills\\r\\n\\r\\nEverything is a skill — **there are no slash commands** (decided 2026-07-17: one kind, one home, one activation path). The catalog at `skills/` and `agents/` is inert storage; nothing auto-loads. An item runs only once **activated by copy** (not symlink) into `.agents/skills/` (Claude: `.claude/skills/`). Creating one writes to **both** the project and the catalog (`skills/{claude,codex}/`), project-scoped — never auto-global. `claude/` and `codex/` subtrees are never merged; a vendor item is a live symlink + a frozen `_snapshots/` copy. `system/scripts/check-skills.sh` validates the model; `skills/INDEX.md` indexes it.\\r\\n\\r\\nActive in this root. Run `pickup` only when Joshua explicitly asks for it. Do not run it automatically at session start. Use `wrapup` at the end of substantive sessions:\\r\\n\\r\\n| Skill | Use it when |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| `pickup` / `wrapup` | `pickup` only on Joshua\'s explicit request / `wrapup` at session end (wrapup persists to the wiki ledgers) |\\r\\n| `session-handoff` | mid-work context reset near ~250k, or passing work Codex↔Claude — chat-only, writes nothing |\\r\\n| `audit` | scoring the OS against the Four Cs (structural health, /100) |\\r\\n| `level-up` | weekly 3Ms interview to find + ship one automation |\\r\\n| `consultation-inquiry-drafts`, `-followup-drafts`, `inbound-tutoring-replies` | drafting to prospects/students — always via the teaching voice |\\r\\n| `guided-drill-assignment` | assigning a student a drill |\\r\\n| `lsat-u-platform-actions` | doing anything ON lsatprep.study from here (assign, post, message, ingest) |\\r\\n| `tutorocean-profile-fill` | TutorOcean profile work |\\r\\n| `prompt-architect-refiner` | refining a prompt before it ships |\\r\\n| `accommodations-email` | \\"accommodations email to X\\" (dictation: \\"accommodations scale\\"); pre-approved send, QP form attached, self-logging |\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen a skill run exposes a gap, improve the skill before moving on. Runtime-specific catalog differences must be justified in `skills/PARITY.md`; undeclared differences are defects.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Guardrails\\r\\n\\r\\n- **95% rule.** Make no changes until you are 95% confident what Joshua wants. Ask until you are.\\r\\n- **Lovable credits require confirmation.** Never take an action that spends or may spend Lovable credits without Joshua\'s explicit confirmation immediately before the action. Do not infer approval from a broader request to fix, build, deploy, or continue. Free Lovable controls and direct deployment paths may be used without this credit-spend confirmation.\\r\\n- **Lovable Cloud deploys through Lovable.** LSAT U currently uses a Lovable Cloud-managed backend. Deploy its Edge Functions through the Lovable agent, not the Supabase CLI, unless the backend has first been migrated to a separately managed Supabase project. Confirm this route with Joshua before starting a Lovable deployment.\\r\\n- **Root stays thin.** Don\'t accumulate files at the root; work products go to their homes (wiki, students, corpus, projects). Anything left at root gets filed before the session ends.\\r\\n- **Delegate bulk work.** Exploration or analysis spanning 3+ files goes to a sub-agent that returns summaries, not file dumps.\\r\\n- **Shell ergonomics.** Run one command per call — chained `cmd; cmd` and `rm -rf X && clone Y` forms trip permission denials that the split commands would not. Never poll with `sleep`. Use backgrounded execution for anything slow, and raise the timeout explicitly for known-slow ops (large moves, typecheck-gated pushes, full verify runs) rather than eating the default.\\r\\n- **Search before creating.** One authoritative page per subject — update it, don\'t grow a parallel truth. `wiki/index.md` catalogs every page and updates on every ingest.\\r\\n- **Preserve provenance and source hashes.** Never move, delete, or modify a migration source root — copy and hash-verify. Never follow a source symlink outside its registered root.\\r\\n- **Conflicts stay explicit until adjudicated** — never silently overwrite one source generation with another.\\r\\n- **Automation writes only generated files or fenced generated sections,** through compare-and-swap.\\r\\n- **Obey nested instruction files** — they win over broader ones for their subtree. LSATU\'s recovered `CLAUDE.md`/`AGENTS.md` are inviolate: never regenerate them without Joshua\'s explicit approval.\\r\\n- **Memory = native automemory.** \\"Remember that…\\" writes one fact per file to the native store; `MEMORY.md` indexes it. This file is the rules; memory is learned facts — never mix them. Transcripts and raw captures never auto-promote.\\r\\n- **Append meaningful system changes** to `wiki/log.md`; log locked decisions to `decisions/log.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Dual-runtime (Codex + Claude)\\r\\n\\r\\n- Begin at the Git-root `AGENTS.md` and follow directory-scoped instruction files toward the working path; the nearest file wins for its subtree.\\r\\n- Keep root instructions inside the runtime byte budget. Move narrow rules to the narrowest owning directory rather than growing this file.\\r\\n- Activated skills are **copies** in `.agents/skills/` (from `skills/codex/`); agent definitions convert to `.codex/agents/*.toml` with the instructions in `developer_instructions`.\\r\\n- Claude reads `CLAUDE.md` and never this file. Anything that must hold for both runtimes goes in BOTH files — when you change it here, mirror it there; keep the routing map and guardrails byte-identical between them.\\r\\n- Activation is unequal by design: Codex reads copies in `.agents/skills/`, Claude reads copies in `.claude/skills/`. Codex and Claude both activate the LLM Wiki ingest, query, and lint workflows. Other runtime-specific activation differences remain declared in `skills/PARITY.md`. Never have separate runtimes writing the same page concurrently.\\r\\n- Never have Claude and Codex editing the same file at once; pass work between them by session handoff (summarize → clear → paste). At ~250k tokens, hand off rather than riding autocompact.\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 11.6 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"02afc5","wall_time_seconds":0.5492922,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":546,"output":"---\\r\\nname: test-driven-development\\r\\ndescription: Use when implementing any feature or bugfix, before writing implementation code\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Test-Driven Development (TDD)\\r\\n\\r\\n## Overview\\r\\n\\r\\nWrite the test first. Watch it fail. Write minimal code to pass.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Core principle:** If you didn\'t watch the test fail, you don\'t know if it tests the right thing.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Violating the letter of the rules is violating the spirit of the rules.**\\r\\n\\r\\n## When to Use\\r\\n\\r\\n**Always:**\\r\\n- New features\\r\\n- Bug fixes\\r\\n- Refactoring\\r\\n- Behavior changes\\r\\n\\r\\n**Exceptions (ask your human partner):**\\r\\n- Throwaway prototypes\\r\\n- Generated code\\r\\n- Configuration files\\r\\n\\r\\nThinking \\"skip TDD just this once\\"? Stop. That\'s rationalization.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Anti-Pattern: Horizontal Slices\\r\\n\\r\\n**DO NOT write all tests first, then all implementation.** This is \\"horizontal slicing\\" — treating RED as \\"write all tests\\" and GREEN as \\"write all code.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nThis produces **crap tests**:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Tests written in bulk test _imagined_ behavior, not _actual_ behavior\\r\\n- You end up testing the _shape_ of things (data structures, function signatures) rather than user-facing behavior\\r\\n- Tests become insensitive to real changes — they pass when behavior breaks, fail when behavior is fine\\r\\n- You outrun your headlights, committing to test structure before understanding the implementation\\r\\n\\r\\n**Correct approach:** Vertical slices via tracer bullets. One test → one implementation → repeat. Each test responds to what you learned from the previous cycle.\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nWRONG (horizontal):\\r\\n  RED:   test1, test2, test3, test4, test5\\r\\n  GREEN: impl1, impl2, impl3, impl4, impl5\\r\\n\\r\\nRIGHT (vertical):\\r\\n  RED→GREEN: test1→impl1\\r\\n  RED→GREEN: test2→impl2\\r\\n  RED→GREEN: test3→impl3\\r\\n  ...\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nSee [tests.md](tests.md) for good/bad test examples, [mocking.md](mocking.md) for mocking guidelines, and [refactoring.md](refactoring.md) for refactor candidates.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The Iron Law\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nNO PRODUCTION CODE WITHOUT A FAILING TEST FIRST\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nWrite code before the test? Delete it. Start over.\\r\\n\\r\\n**No exceptions:**\\r\\n- Don\'t keep it as \\"reference\\"\\r\\n- Don\'t \\"adapt\\" it while writing tests\\r\\n- Don\'t look at it\\r\\n- Delete means delete\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"9c3961","wall_time_seconds":0.6084069,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":965,"output":"---\\r\\nname: site-teardown\\r\\ndescription: \\"Reverse engineer any website into a complete build blueprint. Analyzes the HTML structure, fetches and extracts all animation/interaction code from the site\'s JavaScript, and pulls the complete design system from the CSS. Outputs a structured teardown document with tech stack, every visual effect and how it\'s implemented, design tokens, assets needed, and a section-by-section build plan. Use this skill whenever the user wants to analyze how a website was built, reverse engineer a site, clone or recreate a website, understand what tech stack or animations a site uses, do a site teardown, deconstruct a page, figure out how an effect works on a website, or says things like \'steal this website\', \'break down this site\', \'how did they build this\', \'website blueprint\', \'analyze this site for me\', or gives you a URL and asks you to figure out how it works. Also trigger when the user pastes raw HTML source code and asks what\'s going on or how to recreate it.\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Site Teardown\\r\\n\\r\\nReverse engineer any website into a complete build blueprint — tech stack, every visual effect with implementation details, full design system, and a section-by-section build plan. The output should be thorough enough that someone could hand it to a fresh agent session and build a clone without ever visiting the original site.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When This Skill Activates\\r\\n\\r\\n**Activate when the user wants to:**\\r\\n- Analyze how a website was built\\r\\n- Reverse engineer or deconstruct a site\\r\\n- Clone or recreate a website\'s design and animations\\r\\n- Understand what tech stack, libraries, or animation techniques a site uses\\r\\n- Get a build blueprint for a website they want to recreate\\r\\n- Understand how a specific effect on a website works (and the site hasn\'t been analyzed yet)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Example triggers:**\\r\\n- \\"Tear down this site: https://example.com\\"\\r\\n- \\"How did they build this website?\\"\\r\\n- \\"Analyze this site for me\\"\\r\\n- \\"Reverse engineer this page\\"\\r\\n- \\"I want to steal this website\'s design\\"\\r\\n- \\"Clone this site analysis\\"\\r\\n- \\"Website blueprint for [URL]\\"\\r\\n- \\"Break down this page\\"\\r\\n- \\"Deconstruct this site\\"\\r\\n- `/site-teardown`\\r\\n- `/site-teardown [URL]`\\r\\n- User pastes raw HTML and asks how to recreate it\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pipeline\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 1 — Get the HTML\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML is the foundation. It tells you what\'s on the page — the elements, the classes, the structure, the image/video paths, the SVGs.\\r\\n\\r\\n**If the user pastes raw HTML source code:**\\r\\n- Use it directly. Pasted HTML is more complete than a WebFetch for large pages because WebFetch processes content through a sub-model that may summarize details.\\r\\n- Scan the HTML and extract key structural information:\\r\\n  - All section/component class names\\r\\n  - Image and video source paths\\r\\n  - SVG elements (logos, icons, decorative shapes)\\r\\n  - Form structures\\r\\n  - Navigation structure\\r\\n  - Meta tags (author, description, title)\\r\\n  - Any inline styles or inline scripts\\r\\n\\r\\n**If the user only provides a URL:**\\r\\n- Use WebFetch on the URL with this prompt: \\"Return the complete HTML structure of this page. Include all class names, element hierarchy, image/video src paths, SVG elements, meta tags, script src paths, stylesheet href paths, and any inline styles or scripts. Preserve the exact class names and data attributes. Do not summarize the structure — return as much detail as possible.\\"\\r\\n- Note to user: for the most complete analysis, they can paste the raw HTML (Ctrl+U → Ctrl+A → Ctrl+C on the page).\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 2 — Find the JS and CSS File Paths\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the HTML, locate the main application JavaScript and CSS files. These are what contain the actual behavior and styling.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Look for:**\\r\\n```html\\r\\n<!-- CSS - usually in <head> -->\\r\\n<link rel=\\"stylesheet\\" href=\\"/path/to/main.css\\">\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- JS - usually before </body> -->\\r\\n<script src=\\"/path/to/main.js\\"></script>\\r\\n"}'}]

text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"@'\nimport json,pathlib,hashlib,collections\np=pathlib.Path('audits/skills-2026-10-09'); rows=[]\nfor n in ['claude-review.json','claude-shard-review.json','claude-resume-review.json','codex-gemini-review.json']:\n for x in json.loads((p/n).read_text(encoding='utf-8-sig')):\n  f=pathlib.Path(x['path']); old=x.get('hash',x.get('sha256','')).lower(); new=hashlib.sha256(f.read_bytes()).hexdigest()\n  if old!=new: rows.append((n,x['name'],old,new))\nprint('DRIFT',rows)\nprint('catalog count',[(r,len(list(pathlib.Path('skills',r).glob('*/SKILL.md')))) for r in ['claude','codex','gemini']])\nprint('supplemental excluded SKILL totals',[(r,len(list(pathlib.Path('skills',r).rglob('SKILL.md')))-len(list(pathlib.Path('skills',r).glob('*/SKILL.md')))) for r in ['claude','codex','gemini']])\n'@ | & 'C:/Users/Joshu/AppData/Local/Programs/Python/Python312/python.exe' -",max_output_tokens:1500}));
